The Goldman Symplectic form on the PGL ⁡ ( V ) \PGL(V) -Hitchin component Thanks: ZS was partially supported by the Luxembourg National Research Fund(FNR) AFR bilateral grant COALAS 11802479-2. TZ was partially supported by the National Science Foundation under agreements DMS-1536017, and by the NUS-MOE grant R-146-000-270-133 and A-8000458-00-00. The authors acknowledge support from U.S. National Science Foundation grants DMS 1107452, 1107263, 1107367 “RNMS: GEometric structures And Representation varieties" (the GEAR Network).
Zhe Sun, Tengren Zhang

Abstract
. This article is the second of a pair of articles about the Goldman symplectic form on Hit V ⁡ ( S ) \Hit_{V}(S) , the PGL ⁡ ( V ) \PGL(V) -Hitchin component of a closed, connected, oriented, hyperbolic surface S S . We show that any ideal triangulation 𝒯 \mathcal{T} on S S and any compatible bridge system 𝒥 \mathcal{J} determine a symplectic trivialization of the tangent bundle to the PGL ⁡ ( V ) \PGL(V) -Hitchin component of S S . Using this, we prove that a large class of vector fields defined in the companion paper [ SWZ20 ] , called the ( 𝒯 , 𝒥 ) (\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}) -parallel vector fields, are Hamiltonian. We also show that if ( 𝒯 , 𝒥 ) (\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}) is subordinate to a pants decomposition 𝒫 \mathcal{P} of S S , then the special ( 𝒯 , 𝒥 ) (\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}) -parallel vector fields constructed in the companion paper [ SWZ20 ] give a symplectic basis of the tangent space to Hit V ⁡ ( S ) \Hit_{V}(S) at every point in Hit V ⁡ ( S ) \Hit_{V}(S) . This is then used to prove that the explicit global coordinate system defined in the companion paper [ SWZ20 ] is a global Darboux coordinate system for the PGL ⁡ ( V ) \PGL(V) -Hitchin component.



1.
Introduction

Let
S
be a closed, oriented, connected, hyperbolic surface, and let
\Gamma
denote the fundamental group of
S
. The
Teichmüller space
of
S
, denoted
\mathcal{T}(S)
, is the deformation space of hyperbolic structures on
S
. By considering the holonomy representations of these hyperbolic structures, one can identify
\mathcal{T}(S)
as the connected component

\left\{[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}\big(\Gamma,\PGL(2,\mathbb{R})\big):\rho\text{ is discrete and faithful}\right\}

of
\mathcal{X}\big(\Gamma,\PGL(2,\mathbb{R})\big):=\Hom\big(\Gamma,\PGL(2,\mathbb{R})\big)/\PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
.

This description of
\mathcal{T}(S)
as representations admits a natural generalization where we replace
\PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
with a higher rank, semisimple Lie group
G
that is real split. These generalizations of
\mathcal{T}(S)
are known as the
G
-
Hitchin components
, and are central objects of investigation in the study of higher Teichmüller theory. In this article, we consider the setting when
G=\PGL(V)
, where
V
is an
n
-dimensional real vector space (see Section
2.1
). The
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin component, denoted
\Hit_{V}(S)
, was first studied by Hitchin
[
Hit92
]
, who showed that
\Hit_{V}(S)
is real-analytically diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)}
. Later, Labourie
[
Lab06
]
proved that every representation in
\Hit_{V}(S)
is Borel-Anosov, which implies in particular that these representations are all quasi-isometric embeddings from
\Gamma
(with any word metric) to
\PGL(V)
(with any left-invariant Riemannian metric).

By specializing a general construction due to Goldman
[
Gol84
]
, one can equip
\Hit_{V}(S)
with a natural symplectic structure (see Section
2.5
), known as the
Goldman symplectic form
. Goldman also proved
[
Gol86
]
that when
V=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this symplectic form is a multiple of the more classical Weil-Petersson symplectic form on
\mathcal{T}(S)
. The Goldman symplectic form on
\Hit_{V}(S)
has been an object of interest in the last few years, and was previously studied by many other authors, including
[
Bri15
]
,
[
LW18
]
,
[
Lab18
]
,
[
Nie13
]
,
[
Sun17
]
,
[
Sun21
]
.

Perhaps the most famous result regarding this symplectic form on
\mathcal{T}(S)
is a theorem of Wolpert
[
Wol82
]
,
[
Wol83
]
, which states that for any pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
on
S
, the Fenchel-Nielsen coordinate functions associated to
\mathcal{P}
are global Darboux coordinates for the Weil-Petersson symplectic form. In other words, the Hamiltonian vector fields of the Fenchel-Nielsen coordinate functions corresponding to
\mathcal{P}
are commuting vector fields that give a symplectic basis of
T_{[\rho]}\mathcal{T}(S)
for every
[\rho]\in\mathcal{T}(S)
. This was partially generalized to
\Hit_{V}(S)
when
\dim(V)=3
by Kim
[
Kim99
]
and Choi-Jung-Kim
[
CJK20
]
, and when
\dim(V)=4
by H.T. Jung (in preparation). The key tool used to prove these results is known as Fox calculus, which is a technique that allows one to compute the Goldman Poisson pairing between certain types of functions on
\Hit_{V}(S)
. However, the computations required to implement Fox calculus become exceedingly complicated as
\dim(V)
gets large.

This article is the second of a pair of articles about the Goldman symplectic form on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, where the objective is to generalize Wolpert’s theorem to
\Hit_{V}(S)
for general
V
.

1.1.
Statement of results

In the companion article, Sun, Wienhard, and Zhang
[
SWZ20
]
showed that that given any ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
on
S
and any compatible bridge system
\mathcal{J}
(see Section
3
for definitions), one can construct a family of real-analytic vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
using the geometry of the representations in
\Hit_{V}(S)
. These are called the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields
(
[
SWZ20
, Section 5.1]
), and satisfy the following properties:

•

Any pair of
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields commute.

•

For any
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
and any
v\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, there is a unique
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{X}([\rho])=v
.

Special cases of these flows were previously constructed by Wienhard-Zhang
[
WZ17
]
.

Furthermore, by modifying Bonahon and Dreyer’s
[
BD14
]
parameterization of
\Hit_{V}(S)
, Sun, Wienhard, and Zhang
[
SWZ20
, Theorem 1.1]
also constructed a real-analytic diffeomorphism

\Omega=\Omega_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathscr{C},

where
\mathscr{C}
is a convex polyhedral cone of dimension
(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)
in a real vector space
\mathscr{W}
, such that the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
are exactly the vector fields that are identified via the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization

{\rm d}\Omega:T\Hit_{V}(S)\to T\mathscr{C}\cong\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{W}

to the constant vector fields on
\mathscr{C}
.

The first goal of this article is to prove that
{\rm d}\Omega
is a symplectic trivialization with respect to the Goldman symplectic form.

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
6.1
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an ideal triangulation on
S
and let
\mathcal{J}
be a compatible bridge system. There is a symplectic bilinear form
\omega_{\mathscr{W}}
on the vector space
\mathscr{W}
for which the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization
{\rm d}\Omega
is symplectic. Equivalently, if
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
are
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, then the function
\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

[\rho]\mapsto\omega_{[\rho]}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho]))

is constant.

Theorem
1.1
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 1.2

(Corollary
6.2
)
.

Any
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field is a Hamiltonian vector field on
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

When
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
is subordinate to some pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of
S
, Sun, Wienhard, and Zhang
[
SWZ20
]
specified a particular collection of
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, called the
special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields
, see Definition
7.8
. These consist of

•

\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}

eruption fields
for each pair of pants of
\mathcal{P}
,

•

\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}

hexagon fields
for each pair of pants of
\mathcal{P}
,

•

n-1

twist fields
for each simple closed curve in
\mathcal{P}
,

•

n-1

length fields
for each simple closed curve in
\mathcal{P}
.

In total, there are
(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)
special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent parallel vector fields. If we specialize to the case when
\dim(V)=2
, i.e. when
\Hit_{V}(S)=\mathcal{T}(S)
, then there are no eruption and hexagon fields, but there is one twist field and one length field for each simple closed curve in
\mathcal{P}
, and the twist field integrates to the Fenchel-Nielsen twist flow.

The second goal of this article is to use Theorem
1.1
to prove that the special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields form a symplectic basis at every point in
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Theorem 1.3

(Corollary
7.10
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
be subordinate to a pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of
S
. For any
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, the set

\{\mathcal{X}([\rho])\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S):\mathcal{X}\text{ is a special }(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})\text{-parallel vector field}\}

is a symplectic basis of
T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Remark 1.4
.

There is a small mistake (a missing factor of
2
) in the description of the length vector fields in the companion paper
[
SWZ20
]
. See Definition
7.6
for the correct description of the length vector fields used in Theorem
1.3
to hold, and see Remark
7.7
for a description of the difference between the length vector fields described in
[
SWZ20
]
and the length vector fields described here.

In the companion paper
[
SWZ20
]
, the authors also constructed, for every choice of
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
that is subordinate to a pants decomposition of
S
, a global coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
by associating to every special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
a coordinate function
H(\mathcal{X})
of this coordinate system, which is explicitly given by projective invariants that can be read off from the Frenet curves associated to the
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations. As a consequence of Theorem
1.3
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 1.5
.

The global coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
defined in the companion paper
[
SWZ20
, Corollary 8.15]
is a global Darboux coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

When
\dim(V)=2
, this coordinate system recovers the Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates on
\mathcal{T}(S)
. This thus generalizes Wolpert’s theorem to
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

1.2.
Proof strategy for Theorem
1.1

Behind the proof of Theorem
1.1
is a new method to compute the Goldman symplectic form on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, which is similar in flavor to the techniques used by Bonahon-Sozen
[
BS01
]
on
\mathcal{T}(S)
. This method uses the following theorem obtained by combining the work of Labourie
[
Lab06
]
and Guichard
[
Gui08
]
.

Theorem 1.6

(Guichard, Labourie)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}(V)
denote the space of complete flags in
V
. There is canonical bijection

\Hit_{V}(S)\simeq\left\{\xi:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}\xi\text{ is Frenet and }\rho\text{-equivariant }\\
\text{for some }\rho\in\Hom\big(\Gamma,\PGL(V)\big)\end{array}\right\}\bigg/\PGL(V)

In other words, instead of thinking of
\Hit_{V}(S)
as a space of conjugacy classes of representations, one can think of
\Hit_{V}(S)
as a space of projective classes of Frenet curves (see Definition
2.5
) satisfying an equivariance property.

We now briefly describe this new computational method. Let
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
and let
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
. Fix a choice of
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
and a choice of representative
\rho\in[\rho]
. First, we use the Frenet curves point of view for Hitchin representations to explicitly define a cocycle

\nu_{X}=\nu_{X,\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}\in C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

called the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-
tangent cocycle
of
X
, see Sections
4.2
and
4.3
. We then prove that the assignment

\Psi=\Psi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

given by
X\mapsto\nu_{X}
is a linear injection, so its image
\mathscr{T}=\mathscr{T}(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
is a linear subspace of
C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
of dimension
(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)
. We then show, see Proposition
4.9
, that the induced map

T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

given by
X\mapsto[\Psi(X)]
agrees precisely with the linear isomorphism
T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\simeq H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
introduced by Weil
[
Wei64
]
, which Goldman uses to define the Goldman symplectic form. In particular, each cohomology class in
H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
admits a unique cocycle representative in
\mathscr{T}
.

Then, we explicitly define another linear isomorphism

\Xi:=\Xi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:\mathscr{T}\to\mathscr{W},

see Section
5.3
and Proposition
5.13
. One can think of
\Xi
as abstracting certain “coefficient data" from every
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle. We then prove, see Theorem
5.2
, that for any point
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
and any representative
\rho\in[\rho]
, the map

\Xi\circ\Psi:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathscr{W}\cong T_{\Omega([\rho])}\mathscr{C}

agrees with
{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}
. In particular,
\Xi\circ\Psi
does not depend on the choice of
\rho\in[\rho]
, and the map
[\rho]\mapsto\Xi\circ\Psi(\mathcal{X}([\rho]))
is constant for any
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
on
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Using the explicit descriptions of the
\Psi
and
\Xi
, as well as a well-chosen triangulation of
S
that is compatible with
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
, see Section
6.1
, we may apply the formula (see (
2.9
)) for the Goldman symplectic form to show that for any pair of
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
on
\Hit_{V}(S)
and any
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, the Goldman symplectic pairing at
[\rho]
between
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho])
and
\mathcal{X}([\rho])
depends only on
\Xi\circ\Psi(\mathcal{X}([\rho]))
and
\Xi\circ\Psi(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]))
, which are vectors in
\mathscr{W}
that do not depend on
[\rho]
. Theorem
1.1
follows from this.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we discuss the relationship between Frenet curves and Hitchin representations, as well as some well-known projective invariants such as the cross ratio and triple ratio. Then in Section
3
, we formally define ideal triangulations, barriers systems, and bridge systems. Using this, we define the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles and the map
\Psi=\Psi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}
in Section
4
, and we define the map
\Xi=\Xi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}
in Section
5
. Then in Section
6
and Section
7
, we prove Theorem
1.1
, and Theorem
1.3
respectively.

2.
Features of Hitchin representations and the Hitchin component

In this section, we recall the definition of
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations and the
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin component. We first explain results by Labourie
[
Lab06
]
and Guichard
[
Gui08
]
relating
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations to Frenet curves, as well as results due to Fock and Goncharov
[
FG06
]
regarding the positive nature of
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations. Then, we use a regularity result of Bridgeman, Canary, Labourie, and Sambarino
[
BCLS15
]
to give an explicit description of the real-analytic structure on the
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin component. Finally, we recall Weil’s
[
Wei64
]
cohomological description of the tangent space to the
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin component, as well as the definition of the Goldman symplectic form
[
Gol84
]
on the
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin component.

2.1.
Frenet curves and
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations

Recall that
V
denotes an
n
-dimensional real vector space,
S
denotes a closed, connected, oriented topological surface of genus
g\geq 2
,
\Gamma
denotes the fundamental group of
S
, and
\partial\Gamma
denotes the Gromov boundary of
\Gamma
. It is well-known that
\partial\Gamma
is topologically a circle, and the orientation on
S
induces a (clockwise) cyclic ordering on
\partial\Gamma
.

Definition 2.1
.

A representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
is a
\PGL(V)
-
Hitchin representation
if it can be continuously deformed in
\Hom(\Gamma,\PGL(V))
to a faithful representation whose image is a discrete subgroup that lies in the image of an irreducible representation from
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
\PGL(V)
.

Remark 2.2
.

Since
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is connected, the image of any
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation necessarily lies in the identity component of
\PGL(V)
, namely
\PSL(V)
. As such, these are also sometimes called
\PSL(V)
-Hitchin representations.

Let
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
denotes the set of Hitchin representations in
\Hom(\Gamma,\PGL(V))
. Since
\PGL(V)
is a real algebraic group,
\Hom(\Gamma,\PGL(V))
is naturally a real algebraic variety. It is clear that in the the compact-open topology on
\Hom(\Gamma,\PGL(V))
,
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
is a union of connected components of
\Hom(\Gamma,\PGL(V))
. Furthermore, since the condition for being a
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation is invariant under conjugation by elements in
\PGL(V)
, we can consider the quotient
\Hit_{V}(S):=\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)/\PGL(V)
. This was first studied by Hitchin
[
Hit92
]
, who used Higgs bundle techniques to prove that
\Hit_{V}(S)
is homeomorphic to a cell of dimension
(2g-2)(n^{2}-1)
. In particular,
\Hit_{V}(S)
is connected, and is commonly known as the
\PGL(V)
-
Hitchin component
.

Remark 2.3
.

The quotient
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)/\PSL(V)
has two connected components when
n
is even, but is connected when
n
is odd. Since we want to work with one Hitchin component, it is more convenient to take the quotient
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)/\PGL(V)
.

Note that
\Hit_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(S)
is exactly the space of discrete and faithful representations from
\Gamma
to
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
considered up to conjugation in
\PGL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Every such conjugacy class is the holonomy of a unique hyperbolic structure on
S
, so
\Hit_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(S)
can be naturally identified with the deformation space of hyperbolic structures on
S
, also known as the Fricke-Teichmüller space. This phenomena was generalized by Choi-Goldman
[
CG93
]
, who proved that the
\PGL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin representations are exactly holonomies of convex real projective structures on
S
. However, the geometric properties of
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations in general were poorly understood until a breakthrough by Labourie
[
Lab06
]
, who used Frenet curves to study them. We will now give a brief account of Labourie’s result.

A
(complete) flag

F
in
V
is a nested sequence of subspaces

F^{(1)}\subset F^{(2)}\subset\dots\subset F^{(n-1)}\subset V

so that
\dim F^{(i)}=i
. Let
\mathcal{F}(V)
denote the space of flags in
V
. The space
\mathcal{F}(V)
naturally has the structure of a real-analytic manifold, and
\PGL(V)
acts transitively and real-analytically on
\mathcal{F}(V)
. We say that a
k
-tuple of flags
(F_{1},\dots,F_{k})
in
\mathcal{F}(V)^{k}
is
generic
if
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}+\dots+F_{k}^{(i_{k})}=V
for all non-negative integers
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
that sum to
n
. Let
\mathcal{F}(V)^{[k]}
denote the space of generic
k
-tuples of flags. Note that this is an open set in
\mathcal{F}(V)^{k}
, and so naturally inherits a real-analytic structure which descends to a real-analytic structure on
\mathcal{F}(V)^{[k]}/\PGL(V)
.

Remark 2.4
.

We will repeatedly use the following two observations.

(1)

Let
(F,G),(F^{\prime},G^{\prime})
be generic pairs of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
, and let
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}(V)
so that

p+F^{(i)}+G^{(n-i-1)}=V=p^{\prime}+F^{\prime(i)}+G^{\prime(n-i-1)}

for all
i=0,\dots,n-1
. Then there is a unique projective transformation
g\in\PGL(V)
such that
g\cdot F=F^{\prime}
,
g\cdot G=G^{\prime}
and
g\cdot p=p^{\prime}
.

(2)

Let
F,G,G^{\prime}
be flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
such that
(F,G)
and
(F,G^{\prime})
are generic. Then there is a unique unipotent projective transformation
u\in\PGL(V)
such that
u\cdot F=F
and
u\cdot G=G^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.5
.

A continuous map
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{F}(V)
is
Frenet
if the following hold for all
k=1,\dots,n
:

•

If
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
is a pairwise distinct
k
-tuple of points in
S^{1}
, then

\big(\xi(x_{1}),\dots,\xi(x_{k})\big)

is a generic
k
-tuple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
.

•

Let
\{(x_{1,i},\dots,x_{k,i})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of pairwise distinct
k
-tuples in
S^{1}
, such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}x_{j,i}=x\in S^{1}
for all
j=1,\dots,k
. Then for any positive integers
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
so that
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=m\leq n
, we have

\lim_{i\to\infty}\xi(x_{1,i})^{(n_{1})}+\dots+\xi(x_{k,i})^{(n_{k})}=\xi^{(m)}(x).

Remark 2.6
.

As a consequence of Remark
2.4
(1), given any two Frenet curves
\xi,\xi^{\prime}:S^{1}\to\mathcal{F}(V)
and any triple of pairwise distinct points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in S^{1}
, there is a unique projective transformation
g\in\PGL(V)
such that

g\cdot\left(\xi(x_{1}),\xi(x_{2}),\xi^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)=\left(\xi^{\prime}(x_{1}),\xi^{\prime}(x_{2}),{\xi^{\prime}}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right).

In particular, if
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are projectively equivalent, then
g\cdot\xi=\xi^{\prime}
.

Since
\partial\Gamma
is a topological circle, it makes sense to say whether a continuous map
\xi:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)
is Frenet. Labourie proved that every
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
preserves a unique
\rho
-equivariant Frenet map
\xi_{\rho}:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)
. Using this, he deduces that every
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation is an irreducible quasi-isometric embedding (with respect to any word metric on
\Gamma
and any left-invariant Riemannian metric on
\PGL(V)
). He also conjectured that for any representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
, the existence of such a
\rho
-equivariant Frenet map implies that
\rho
is a
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation. This was later proven by Guichard, thus establishing the following theorem.

Theorem 2.7
.

[
Gui08
, Theorem 1]
,
[
Lab06
, Theorem 1.4]

A representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
is a
\PGL(V)
-
Hitchin representation
if and only if there exists a
\rho
-equivariant Frenet map
\xi_{\rho}:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)
. If such a
\rho
-equivariant Frenet map exists, then it is unique. Furthermore, for any pair
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
of
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations,
\xi_{\rho}=\xi_{\rho^{\prime}}
if and only if
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
.

In particular, this establishes a natural bijection between
\Hit_{V}(S)
and the set of projective classes of Frenet curves
\xi:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)
that are
\rho
-equivariant for some representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
. At about the same time, Fock and Goncharov
[
FG06
]
also gave a more algebraic version of the criterion in Theorem
2.7
, where they replace the Frenet property with the notion of a “positive map".

As a consequence of Theorem
2.7
, Labourie deduces the following.

Corollary 2.8
.

[
Lab06
, Proposition 3.2]

Let
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
be a
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations with
\xi_{\rho}:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)
as its
\rho
-equivariant map. Then for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
that is not the identity,
\rho(\gamma)
has
\xi_{\rho}(\gamma^{-})
and
\xi_{\rho}(\gamma^{+})
as its attracting and repelling fixed point in
\mathcal{F}(V)
, where
\gamma^{-}
and
\gamma^{+}
are the attracting and repelling fixed point of
\gamma
in
\partial\Gamma
. Furthermore, any (some) linear representative of
\rho(\gamma)
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{R}
with pairwise distinct eigenvalues, all of which have the same sign.

2.2.
Positivity of
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations

The description of Hitchin representations via Frenet curves allows one to prove a striking positivity feature for Hitchin representations, which was first observed by Fock and Goncharov
[
FG06
]
. To describe this, we will define two well-known projective invariants associated to transverse pairs and triples of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
.

Let
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}
be a triple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
. For
m=1,2,3
, choose a basis
\{f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,n}\}
of
V
such that
F_{m}^{(j)}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,j})
for all
j=1,\dots,n-1
. For each triple
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of non-negative integers that are weakly between
0
and
n-1
, and that sum to
n
, define

F_{1}^{(i_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2})}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3})}:=\det(f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}).

If
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
is a generic triple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
, then
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2})}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3})}\neq 0
.

Definition 2.9
.

(1)

Let
F_{1},G_{1},F_{2},G_{2}
be flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
so that
(F_{1},G_{1},F_{2})
and
(F_{1},F_{2},G_{2})
are triples of generic flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
. For any pair
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, define the
cross ratio
by

C^{\mathbf{k}}(F_{1},G_{1},F_{2},G_{2}):=\frac{F_{1}^{(k_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(k_{2}-1)}\wedge G_{2}^{(1)}}{F_{1}^{(k_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(k_{2}-1)}\wedge G_{1}^{(1)}}\cdot\frac{F_{1}^{(k_{1}-1)}\wedge F_{2}^{(k_{2})}\wedge G_{1}^{(1)}}{F_{1}^{(k_{1}-1)}\wedge F_{2}^{(k_{2})}\wedge G_{2}^{(1)}}.

(2)

Let
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
be a triple of generic flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
. For any triple
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, define the
triple ratio

\displaystyle T^{\mathbf{i}}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}):=\frac{F_{1}^{(i_{1}+1)}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2})}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3}-1)}}{F_{1}^{(i_{1}+1)}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2}-1)}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3})}}\cdot

\displaystyle\frac{F_{1}^{(i_{1}-1)}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3})}}{F_{1}^{(i_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3}-1)}}

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{F_{1}^{(i_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2}-1)}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3}+1)}}{F_{1}^{(i_{1}-1)}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2})}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3}+1)}}.

The definition of
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}\wedge F_{2}^{(i_{2})}\wedge F_{3}^{(i_{3})}
depends on the bases
\{f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,n}\}
for
m=1,2,3
, but one can easily verify that the cross ratio and triple ratio do not. Another important but easily verified property of the cross ratio and triple ratio is their projective invariance, i.e. for any
g\in\PGL(V)
,

•

C^{\mathbf{k}}(F_{1},G_{1},F_{2},G_{2})=C^{\mathbf{k}}(g\cdot F_{1},g\cdot G_{1},g\cdot F_{2},g\cdot G_{2})
for all
\mathbf{k}
, and

•

T^{\mathbf{i}}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})=T^{\mathbf{i}}(g\cdot F_{1},g\cdot G_{2},g\cdot F_{3})
for all
\mathbf{i}
.

It is also easy to check the following symmetries from the definition:

•

For any pair of positive integers
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
that sum to
n
, let
\bar{\mathbf{k}}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
. Then

(2.1)

\displaystyle C^{\mathbf{k}}(F_{1},G_{1},F_{2},G_{2})=C^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}(F_{2},G_{2},F_{1},G_{1})=\frac{1}{C^{\mathbf{k}}(F_{1},G_{2},F_{2},G_{1})}.

•

For any triple
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, let
\mathbf{i}_{+}:=(i_{2},i_{3},i_{1})
, let
\mathbf{i}_{-}:=(i_{3},i_{1},i_{2})
, and let
\bar{\mathbf{i}}:=(i_{3},i_{2},i_{1})
, where the arithmetic in the subscripts are done modulo
3
. Then

(2.2)

\displaystyle T^{\mathbf{i}}(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})=T^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}(F_{2},F_{3},F_{1})=T^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}(F_{3},F_{1},F_{2})=\frac{1}{T^{\bar{\mathbf{i}}}(F_{3},F_{2},F_{1})}.

For any pairwise distinct triple
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
in
S^{1}
and any Frenet curve
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{F}(V)
,
\xi(\mathbf{x})
is a generic triple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
. Thus, we can evaluate cross ratios and triple ratios along the image of
\xi
. The following theorem is due to Fock-Goncharov
[
FG06
]
(also see
[
LM09
, Appendix B]
or
[
Zha15
, Proposition 2.5.7]
).

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\xi:S^{1}\to\mathcal{F}(V)
be a Frenet curve, and equip
S^{1}
with the clockwise cyclic order.

(1)

For any pairwise distinct triple of points
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
in
S^{1}
, and for any triple
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, we have

T^{\mathbf{i}}\big(\xi(\mathbf{x})\big)>0.

(2)

For any pairwise distinct quadruple of points
r_{1},s_{1},r_{2},s_{2}\in S^{1}
such that
r_{1}<s_{1}<r_{2}<s_{2}<r_{1}
in the cyclic ordering on
S^{1}
, and for any pair
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, we have

C^{\mathbf{k}}\big(\xi(r_{1}),\xi(s_{1}),\xi(r_{2}),\xi(s_{2})\big)<0.

2.3.
Real-analytic structure of the
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations

It is a straightforward consequence of Theorem
2.7
that the centralizer of every
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation is trivial, so by Goldman
[
Gol84
, Proposition 1.2]
,
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
lies in the smooth locus of the real algebraic variety
\Hom(\Gamma,\PGL(V))
. As such,
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
is naturally equipped with a real-analytic structure. This real-analytic structure has the defining property that for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the map

\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to G

given by
\rho\mapsto\rho(\gamma)
is real-analytic.

Theorem
2.7
also implies that every
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation is irreducible, see
[
Gui08
, Proposition 14]
. Thus, by Johnson-Millson
[
JM87
, Section 1]
(also see Goldman
[
Gol84
, Section 1.3]
), the
\PGL(V)
-action on
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
by conjugation is real-analytic and proper, so
\Hit_{V}(S)
has the structure of a real-analytic manifold with the defining property that the quotient map
\pi:\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to\Hit_{V}(S)
is real-analytic. With this real-analytic structure, Hitchin’s proof
[
Hit92
]
in fact shows that
\Hit_{V}(S)
is real-analytically diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{(2g-2)(n^{2}-1)}
(also see an independent proof due to Bonahon-Dreyer
[
BD14
]
).

We will now describe the real-analytic structure on
\Hit_{V}(S)
explicitly by specifying an explicit real-analytic embedded submanifold of
\mathcal{H}\subset\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
(depending on some choices), such that
\pi|_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\to\Hit_{V}(S)
restricted to
\mathcal{H}
is a real-analytic diffeomorphism onto
\Hit_{V}(S)
. This will also be used later in the description of the tangent cocycle in Section
2.4
.

Our description of the real-analytic structure on
\Hit_{V}(S)
relies on the specialization of a result of Bridgeman, Canary, Labourie, and Sambarino
[
BCLS15
]
to
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representations.

Theorem 2.11
.

[
BCLS15
, Theorem 6.1]
For every
x\in\partial\Gamma
, the map

\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to\mathcal{F}(V)

given by
\rho\mapsto\xi_{\rho}(x)
is real-analytic.

We say that a triple
(F,G,p)\in\mathcal{F}(V)^{2}\times\mathbb{P}(V)
is a
normalizing triple
if
(F,G)
is a transverse pair of flags and

p+F^{(i)}+G^{(n-i-1)}=V

for all
i=0,\dots,n-1
. For any normalizing triple
(F,G,p)
and any triple
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
, let

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}((F,G,p),{\bf x}):=\left\{\rho\in\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S):\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)=(F,G,p)\right\}.

For any
g\in\PGL(V)
, let
c_{g}:\PGL(V)\to\PGL(V)
be the map given by
h\mapsto ghg^{-1}
. The following is a corollary of Theorem
2.11
.

Corollary 2.12
.

Fix a normalizing triple
(F,G,p)
and any triple
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
, and let
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}((F,G,p),{\bf x})
.

(1)

\mathcal{H}\subset\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
is an embedded real-analytic submanifold.

(2)

The map
\widetilde{I}:\mathcal{H}\times\PGL(V)\to\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
given by
\widetilde{I}:(\rho,g)\mapsto c_{g}\circ\rho
is a real-analytic diffeomorphism. In particular, the obvious quotient map
\pi:\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to\Hit_{V}(S)
restricts to a real-analytic diffeomorphism
\pi|_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\to\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Proof.

Define the surjective map

f:\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to\PGL(V)

with the defining property that
f(\rho)\cdot(F,G,p)=\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)
. It follows from Theorem
2.11
that
f
is real-analytic.

(1) Since
\mathcal{H}=f^{-1}(\id)
, by the constant rank level set theorem, it suffices to show that
f
is regular, i.e.
{\rm d}f_{\rho}:T_{\rho}\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to T_{f(\rho)}\PGL(V)
is surjective for every
\rho\in\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
. Let
X\in T_{f(\rho)}\PGL(V)
, and let
t\mapsto g_{t}
be a smooth path in
\PGL(V)
such that
g_{0}=f(\rho)
and
\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}g_{t}=X
. Note that
t\mapsto\rho_{t}:=c_{g_{t}g_{0}^{-1}}\circ\rho
is a smooth path in
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
, and
\xi_{\rho_{t}}=g_{t}g_{0}^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\rho}
for all
t
. Thus,

\displaystyle f(\rho_{t})\cdot(F,G,p)

\displaystyle=\left(\xi_{\rho_{t}}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)=g_{t}g_{0}^{-1}\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=g_{t}\cdot(F,G,p),

so
f(\rho_{t})=g_{t}
. This means that
{\rm d}f_{\rho}(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho_{t})=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}f(\rho_{t})=X
, which proves that
f
is regular.

(2) Observe that
\widetilde{I}^{-1}
is given by
\widetilde{I}^{-1}(\rho):=(c_{f(\rho)^{-1}}\circ\rho,f(\rho))
. Since
f
is real-analytic, it follows that
\widetilde{I}
is a real-analytic diffeomorphism. For the second claim, note that
\widetilde{I}
is
\PGL(V)
-equivariant, where the
\PGL(V)
action on
\mathcal{H}\times\PGL(V)
is given by the trivial action on the first factor and left multiplication in the second factor, while the
\PGL(V)
action on
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
is by conjugation. Thus,
\widetilde{I}
descends to a real-analytic diffeomorphism

(2.4)

\displaystyle I:\mathcal{H}\to\Hit_{V}(S).

Observe that
I
associates every
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation in
\mathcal{H}
to its conjugacy class in
\Hit_{V}(S)
, so
I=\pi|_{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Since
\mathcal{H}((F,G,p),{\bf x})
is a real-analytic embedded submanifold of
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
, the following corollary is immediate from Theorem
2.11
.

Corollary 2.13
.

Fix a normalizing triple
(F,G,p)
and any triple
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
, and let
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}((F,G,p),{\bf x})
.

(1)

For any triple
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
and
\rho\in\mathcal{H}
, let
g(\rho)\in\PGL(V)
be defined by

g(\rho)\cdot(F,G,p)=\left(\xi_{\rho}(y_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(y_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right).

Then the map
g:\mathcal{H}\to\PGL(V)
is real-analytic.

(2)

For any point
r
in
\partial\Gamma
distinct from
x_{1}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{H}
, let
u(\rho)\in\PGL(V)
be the unique unipotent projective transformation defined by

u(\rho)\cdot\left(F,G\right)=\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(r)\right).

Then the map
u:\mathcal{H}\to\PGL(V)
is real-analytic.

2.4.
The tangent space to
\Hit_{V}(S)

We now recall a remarkable observation by Weil
[
Wei64
]
(also see Goldman
[
Gol84
, Section 1]
) that gives a chomological description of the tangent space to
\Hit_{V}(S)
at any point
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
. This is essential in order to define the Goldman symplectic form. Although this chomological interpretation holds in much greater generality, we will only describe this for
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Let
\mathfrak{sl}(V)
denote the Lie algebra of
\PGL(V)
and let
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
be a Hitchin representation. We denote the first (group) cohomology of
\Gamma
and the first (singular) cohomology of
S
, both with coefficients in
\mathfrak{sl}(V)
twisted by
\Ad\circ\rho
, by

H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\,\text{ and }\,H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

respectively. We also denote the corresponding real vector spaces of
1
-cocycles by

C^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\,\text{ and }\,C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

respectively.

Weil observed that there is a canonical isomorphism

(2.5)

T_{\rho}\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\simeq C^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho}).

This isomorphism can be described explicitly: Given a tangent vector
X\in T_{\rho}\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
, let
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
be a smooth path in
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho_{t}=X
. Then for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
t\mapsto\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
is a smooth path in
\PGL(V)
. Since
\rho_{t}
is a homomorphism for all
t
, one can verify that the map
\mu_{X}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
defined by

\mu_{X}:\gamma\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{sl}(V)

is a
1
-cocycle in
C^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
, i.e.

\mu_{X}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})=\mu_{X}(\gamma_{1})+\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma_{1})\cdot\mu_{X}(\gamma_{2})

for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
. Then the map
T_{\rho}\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to C^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
given by
X\mapsto\mu_{X}
is the required isomorphism.

Furthermore, via the isomorphism (
2.5
), the coboundaries in
C^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
, i.e.
1
-cocycles of the form

\gamma\mapsto A-\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot A

for some
A\in\mathfrak{sl}(V)
, are identified with the tangent vectors in
T_{\rho}\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
to paths of the form
t\mapsto c_{g_{t}}\circ\rho
for some smooth path
t\mapsto g_{t}
in
\PGL(V)
with
g_{0}=\id
. As such, (
2.5
) descends to an isomorphism

(2.6)

T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\simeq H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho}).

At the same time, there is a well-known isomorphism

(2.7)

H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\simeq H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

which can also be described explicitly. Let
\nu\in C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
be a
1
-cocycle in the cohomology class
[\nu]\in H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
, and let
\widetilde{\nu}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
be its
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift, where
\widetilde{S}
is the universal cover of
S
. Pick any point
o\in\widetilde{S}
. For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, let
h_{\gamma}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be a
1
-simplex such that
h_{\gamma}(0)=o
and
h_{\gamma}(1)=\gamma\cdot o
. Then define
\bar{\nu}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
by
\bar{\nu}:\gamma\mapsto\widetilde{\nu}(h_{\gamma})
. One can verify that
\bar{\nu}
is a well-defined
1
-cocycle in
C^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
, and that the map
H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\to H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
given by
[\nu]\mapsto[\bar{\nu}]
is an isomorphism that does not depend on the choice of
o
.

The composite of the isomorphisms (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) give an isomorphism

(2.8)

\displaystyle F_{\rho}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho}).

This is the required cohomological description of
T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
.

2.5.
The Goldman symplectic form

Recall that the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
of
\PGL(V)
is naturally identified with the traceless endomorphisms of
V
. Hence, one can define the
trace form

B:\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)\times\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)\to\mathbb{R}

by
B(X,Y):=\tr(XY)
. This pairing is well-known to be bilinear, symmetric, non-degenerate, and
\Ad(\PGL(V))
-invariant, i.e. is invariant under conjugation by group elements in
\PGL(V)
. Using the trace form, we apply a general construction by Goldman
[
Gol84
]
to define a symplectic form on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, called the
Goldman symplectic form
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
.

(1)

For any be a representative
\rho
in
[\rho]
, define the pairing

\omega_{\rho}:H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\times H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\to H^{2}(S,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}

where the first map is the cup product on cohomology while pairing the
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho}
coefficients using the trace form, and the second map is taking the cap product with the fundamental class
[S]\in H_{2}(S,\mathbb{R})
(given by the orientation on
S
).

(2)

The
Goldman symplectic form
is the pull-back pairing

\omega_{[\rho]}:=F_{\rho}^{*}(\omega_{\rho}):T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\times T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}.

Using the fact that the trace form
B
is
\Ad(\PGL(V))
-invariant, one can verify that
\omega_{\rho}
is well-defined and
\omega_{[\rho]}
does not depend on the choice of representative
\rho
in
[\rho]
. Also, Goldman
[
Gol84
, Theorem 1.7]
proved that
\omega
is a symplectic form on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, i.e if we view
\omega:[\rho]\mapsto\omega_{[\rho]}
as a
2
-form on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, then
d\omega=0
.

Remark 2.15
.

From the work of Atiyah-Bott
[
AB83
]
, one also has a description of this symplectic form in the language of flat bundles over
S
. In the case when
\dim(V)=2
, Goldman
[
Gol84
, Proposition 2.5]
showed that this symplectic form agrees with (a multiple of) the Weil-Petersson symplectic form on the Teichmüller space of
S
.

One can use singular homology and cohomology to compute the Goldman symplectic form explicitly. To do so, one needs an explicit
2
-cycle that represents the fundamental class
[S]
in
H_{2}(S,\mathbb{R})
. A standard way to specify such a
2
-cycle is to choose a finite triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
S
such that the three vertices of every triangle in
\mathbb{T}
are pairwise distinct, and to choose an enumeration
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
. Then for any triangle
\widehat{\delta}
in
\mathbb{T}
, let
v_{a}
,
v_{b}
, and
v_{c}
be the vertices of
\widehat{\delta}
such that
a<b<c
, let
\sigma_{\widehat{\delta}}
denote the
2
-simplex
[v_{a},v_{b},v_{c}]
in
S
, let
e_{\widehat{\delta},1}
denote the
1
-simplex
[v_{a},v_{b}]
, and let
e_{\widehat{\delta},2}
denote the
1
-simplex
[v_{b},v_{c}]
. One can then verify
[
Mun18
, Chapter 1]
that the fundamental class
[S]
in
H_{2}(S,\mathbb{R})
is represented by the
2
-cycle

\sum_{\widehat{\delta}\in\mathbb{T}}\sgn(\widehat{\delta})\cdot\sigma_{\widehat{\delta}},

where
\sgn(\widehat{\delta})
is
1
if the enumeration of the vertices of
\widehat{\delta}
is increasing in the anti-clockwise order (in the orientation on
S
), and is
-1
otherwise.

Now, for each
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, choose a
1
-cocycle representative

\nu_{X}\in C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

of the cohomology class
F_{\rho}(X)
, and let
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
be the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of
\nu_{X}
. For each triangle
\widehat{\delta}
in
\mathbb{T}
, choose a lift
\delta
to
\widetilde{S}
of
\widehat{\delta}
, and let
\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
denote the set of all such lifts, one for each triangle in
\mathbb{T}
. Then for each triangle
\delta
in
\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
, let
e_{\delta,1}
and
e_{\delta,2}
denote the oriented edges of
\delta
such that
\pi_{S}(e_{\delta,1})=e_{\widehat{\delta},1}
and
\pi_{S}(e_{\delta,2})=e_{\widehat{\delta},2}
, where
\pi_{S}:\widetilde{S}\to S
is the covering map. In light of the
2
-cycle given above that represents the fundamental class in
H_{2}(S,\mathbb{R})
, we see that the Goldman symplectic form is computed by

(2.9)

\omega_{[\rho]}\big(X_{1},X_{2}\big)=\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}}\sgn(\delta)\cdot\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}_{X_{1}}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{X_{2}}(e_{\delta,2})\big),

for any pair of vectors
X_{1},X_{2}\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, where
\sgn(\delta)
is
1
if the enumeration of the vertices of
\pi_{S}(\delta)
is increasing in the anti-clockwise order, and is
-1
otherwise.

3.
Topological constructions

To compute the Goldman symplectic pairing using (
2.9
), one needs to choose, for each
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, a cocycle representative in
C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
for the cohomology class
F_{\rho}(X)\in H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
(the map
F_{\rho}
was defined by (
2.8
)). In general, there is no canonical way to choose such cocycle representatives. Thus, in order to determine a cocycle representative for each
F_{\rho}(X)
, we will have to make some topological choices on
S
. In this section we will describe these topological choices.

Fix once and for all a hyperbolic metric on
S
. We will recall the notion of an (oriented and unoriented) ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
on
S
. Using this, we then define the (oriented and unoriented) barrier systems on
S
associated to
\mathcal{T}
, as well as the bridge systems on
S
compatible with
\mathcal{T}
. Even though we chose a hyperbolic metric on
S
, this is for purely expository reasons; all the notions we define here can be described using only the topology of
S
. However, we do not give these topological descriptions because they are not needed for our purposes.

3.1.
Ideal triangulations on
S

First, we recall the notion of an ideal triangulation on
S
. For more details, see
[
Thu79
,
CB88
,
PH92
,
Bon01
]
.

An
ideal triangulation
on
S
is a collection
\mathcal{T}
of FINITELY many pairwise non-intersecting simple geodesics in
S
, whose union is a closed subset of
S
that cuts
S
into finitely many open sets, each of which is a hyperbolic ideal triangle. We refer to the geodesics in
\mathcal{T}
as the
edges
in
\mathcal{T}
, and refer to the open sets in the complement of
\mathcal{T}
as the
ideal triangles
of
\mathcal{T}
.

There are two possible kinds of edges in
\mathcal{T}
, the bi-infinite ones and the closed ones. The bi-infinite edges are isolated in
S
, i.e. for every bi-infinite edge
e
, there is an open set
U\subset S
containing
e
, such that
U
does not intersect any other edge in
\mathcal{T}
. However, they are not closed as subsets in
S
; they accumulate onto closed geodesics in
\mathcal{T}
in both directions. On the other hand, the closed edges in
S
are closed as subsets of
S
, but they are not isolated; in fact, for every closed edge
e
and every collar neighborhood
U\subset S
containing
e
, each connected component of
U\setminus e
intersects an isolated edge in
\mathcal{T}
. So that these notions still make sense in
\widetilde{S}
, we will refer to the bi-infinite edges in
S
as
isolated edges
, and the closed edges in
S
as
non-isolated edges
. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
to be the set of geodesics in
\widetilde{S}
that are mapped via the covering map
\pi_{S}:\widetilde{S}\to S
to a geodesic in
\mathcal{T}
. We say that an edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is
isolated
(resp.
non-isolated
) if its projection to
S
is isolated (resp. non-isolated). We also refer to the open sets in the complement of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
as the
ideal triangles
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.

Note that the three edges of every ideal triangle of
\mathcal{T}
are necessarily isolated edges. As a consequence of the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, if
g
is the genus of
S
, then there are
4g-4
ideal triangles given by
\mathcal{T}
,
6g-6
isolated edges in
\mathcal{T}
, and (weakly) between
1
and
3g-3
non-isolated edges in
\mathcal{T}
.

Given an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
on
S
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
on
\widetilde{S}
), we define the
associated oriented ideal triangulation
, denoted
\mathcal{T}^{o}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
), to be the set of oriented geodesics in
S
(resp.
\widetilde{S}
) whose underlying non-oriented geodesic lies in
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
).

3.2.
Barrier systems

Next, we define the notion of a barrier system associated to an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
on
S
. Note that for each ideal triangle
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
and each vertex
x
of
T
, there is a unique geodesic ray in
\widetilde{S}
from the centroid of
T
to
x
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
denote the collection of all such geodesic rays, and define
\mathcal{M}
to be the set of geodesic rays in
S
that lift to a geodesic ray in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. We will refer to the geodesic rays in
\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
) as
non-edge barriers associated to
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
)
, see Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
This is a drawing in the Klein model of
\widetilde{S}
of a barrier system
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
associated to an ideal triangulation
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
and a bridge system
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
compatible to
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. The non-isolated edges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
are drawn in thick red, the isolated edges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
are drawn in blue, and the non-edge barriers in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
are drawn in thin yellow. The bridges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
are realized by geodesic segments drawn in dotted green. One ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is shaded violet, and one fragmented ideal triangle is shaded in checkerboard.

Definition 3.1
.

(1)

The
barrier system
in
\widetilde{S}
(resp.
S
) associated to
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}
) is the set
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{M}
). The elements in
\mathcal{D}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
are called
barriers
.

(2)

The
oriented barrier system
associated to
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
) is the set
\mathcal{T}^{o}\cup\mathcal{M}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
), where
\mathcal{T}^{o}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
) is the oriented ideal triangulation associated to
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
), see Section
3.1
.

3.3.
Bridge systems

Finally, we define the notion of a bridge system compatible with an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
S
. To describe this, we use the following notion.

Definition 3.2
.

A
fragmented ideal triangle
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
is a connected component of
\widetilde{S}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, see Figure
1
. We also refer to a connected component of
S\setminus\mathcal{D}
as a
fragmented ideal triangle
of
\mathcal{D}
.

Note that every fragmented ideal triangle
\mathfrak{T}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
lies in a unique ideal triangle
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. Furthermore, the two vertices of
\mathfrak{T}
that lie in
\partial\widetilde{S}
are vertices of
T
, and the vertex of
\mathfrak{T}
that lies in
\widetilde{S}
is the centroid of
T
.

Given a non-isolated edge
e
, we say that a fragmented ideal triangle
\mathfrak{T}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}

faces

e
if

•

one of the vertices of
\mathfrak{T}
is an endpoint of
e
, and

•

the geodesic between the two vertices of
\mathfrak{T}
that lie in
\partial\widetilde{S}
separates
\widetilde{S}
into two connected components, one of which contains
e
and the other contains the third vertex of
\mathfrak{T}
.

With this, we define the notion of a bridge.

Definition 3.3
.

(1)

For any non-isolated edge
e
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, a
bridge
across
e
is a geodesic segment
J
that intersects
e
transversely, and whose endpoints both lie in the interiors of fragmented ideal triangles that face
e
, see Figure
1
.

(2)

A
bridge system compatible with
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is a minimal,
\Gamma
-invariant collection of bridges
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
such that every non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
has at least (equivalently, exactly) one bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across it. If
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
is a bridge system compatible with
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then
\mathcal{J}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}/\Gamma
is a
bridge system compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.

While the barrier system
\mathcal{D}
associated to
\mathcal{T}
is determined by
\mathcal{T}
, there are infinitely many bridge systems that are compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.

4.
Tangent cocycles for the Hitchin component

Recall that we have fixed once and for all a hyperbolic metric on
S
. Given a
\PGL(V)
-Hitchin representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
, an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
on
S
, and a compatible bridge system
\mathcal{J}
, the goal of this section is to define a natural injective linear map

\Psi=\Psi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

with the property that for every
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, the cocycle
\Psi(X)
is a representative of the cohomology class
F_{\rho}(X)\in H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{sl}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
(the map
F_{\rho}
was defined by (
2.8
)). For every
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, we will refer to

\nu_{X}=\nu_{X,\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:=\Psi(X)\in C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

as the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
associated to
X
, and denote the set of
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles (i.e. the image of
\Psi
) by

\mathscr{T}(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})\subset C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{sl}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho}).

These
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles are the cocycle representatives that we will use in the formula (
2.9
) to calculate the Goldman symplectic pairing.

4.1.
Preliminaries to define the tangent cocycle

First, we set up some notation and prove some preliminary results needed to define the tangent cocycle.

Fix a Hitchin representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
, and let
\xi_{\rho}
denote the
\rho
-equivariant Frenet curve. For any triple of pairwise distinct points
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
in
\partial\Gamma
, let

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}:=\left\{\sigma\in\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S):\left(\xi_{\sigma}(x_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(x_{2}),\xi_{\sigma}^{1}(x_{3})\right)=\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{1}(x_{3})\right)\right\}.

Note that
\rho\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
. By Corollary
2.12
,
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
is an embedded real-analytic submanifold of
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
, and if
\pi:\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to\Hit_{V}(S)
is the obvious quotient map, then

\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}\to\Hit_{V}(S)

is a real-analytic diffeomorphism. In particular,

{\rm d}(\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}}^{-1})_{[\rho]}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to T_{\rho}\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}

is a linear isomorphism.

For any triples
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
, we may define the map

(4.2)

\displaystyle g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}\to\PGL(V)

by
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(y_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(y_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)=\left(\xi_{\sigma}(y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(y_{2}),\xi_{\sigma}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)
. Similarly, for any triple
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
and any
q\in\partial\Gamma
distinct from
x_{1}
, we may define the map

(4.3)

\displaystyle u_{{\bf x},q}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}\to\PGL(V)

with the defining property that
u_{{\bf x},q}
is unipotent and
u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(q)\right)=\left(\xi_{\sigma}(x_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(q)\right)
. It follows from Corollary
2.13
that
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}
and
u_{{\bf x},q}
are real-analytic, and it is clear that
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\rho)=\id=u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho)
. The following lemma gives some basic properties of the map
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}
and
u_{{\bf x},q}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
,
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
and
{\bf z}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
be triples of pairwise distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
, let
q\in\partial\Gamma
be distinct from
x_{1}
, and let
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

(1)

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf y}}
are conjugate, then

g_{{\bf x},{\bf z}}(\sigma)=g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)g_{{\bf y},{\bf z}}(\sigma^{\prime}).

In particular,
g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}}(\sigma)=\id
and
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)=g_{{\bf y},{\bf x}}(\sigma^{\prime})^{-1}
.

(2)

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,\gamma\cdot{\bf x}}
are conjugate, then

g_{\gamma\cdot{\bf x},\gamma\cdot{\bf y}}(\sigma^{\prime})=\rho(\gamma)g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}.

(3)

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,\gamma\cdot{\bf x}}
are conjugate, then

u_{\gamma\cdot{\bf x},\gamma\cdot q}(\sigma^{\prime})=\rho(\gamma)u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}.

Proof.

First, we prove (1). Since
\left(\xi_{\rho}(y_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(y_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)=\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(y_{2}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)
, it follows from the definition of
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)
that

\left(\xi_{\sigma}(y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(y_{2}),\xi_{\sigma}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)=g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{(1)}(y_{1})\right),

which implies that
\xi_{\sigma}=g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\cdot\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}
because
\sigma
is conjugate to
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle g_{{\bf x},{\bf z}}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(z_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(z_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(z_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\xi_{\sigma}(z_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(z_{2}),\xi_{\sigma}^{(1)}(z_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(z_{1}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(z_{2}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{(1)}(z_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)g_{{\bf y},{\bf z}}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(z_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(z_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(z_{3})\right),

so (1) holds.

To prove (2) and (3), observe that

\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma\cdot{\bf x})=\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot{\bf x})=\rho(\gamma)\cdot\xi_{\rho}({\bf x})=\rho(\gamma)\cdot\xi_{\sigma}({\bf x})=\rho(\gamma)\sigma(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\sigma}(\gamma\cdot{\bf x}).

Since
\sigma
is conjugate to
\sigma^{\prime}
, this implies that
\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\rho(\gamma)\sigma(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\sigma}
. Thus,

\displaystyle g_{\gamma\cdot{\bf x},\gamma\cdot{\bf y}}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot y_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot y_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot y_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma\cdot y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma\cdot y_{2}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot y_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\sigma}(y_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(y_{2}),\xi_{\sigma}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(y_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(y_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(y_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot y_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot y_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot y_{3})\right),

so (2) holds. Similarly,

\displaystyle u_{\gamma\cdot{\bf x},\gamma\cdot q}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma\cdot q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\sigma}(x_{1}),\xi_{\sigma}(q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(q)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot q)\right),

so (3) holds.
∎

Now, suppose that
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
. Let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
be triples of pairwise distinct points such that both
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
lie in
\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
, and let
q
and
q^{\prime}
denote the points in
\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
that are not
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
respectively. Then define

(4.4)

\displaystyle d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}\to\PGL(V)

by
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma):=u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)^{-1}g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}}(\sigma)u_{{\bf x}^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)
, where
\sigma^{\prime}
is the representation in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}^{\prime}}
that is conjugate to
\sigma
, i.e.

\sigma^{\prime}:=\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}^{\prime}}}^{-1}\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}}(\sigma).

By Corollary
2.13
,
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}
is real-analytic, and it is clear that
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\rho)=\id
. The next lemma gives some basic properties of
d_{{\bf x,}{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be distinct points in
\partial\Gamma
, let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be triples of pairwise distinct points such that both
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
lie in
\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
, and let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be the points in
\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
that are not
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
respectively.

(1)

For any
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
,
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x^{\prime}},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)
fixes both
\xi_{\rho}(r_{1})
and
\xi_{\rho}(r_{2})
. In particular,
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x^{\prime}},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is well-defined.

(2)

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}^{\prime}}
are conjugate, then

d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)^{-1}=d_{{\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf x},q^{\prime},q}(\sigma^{\prime}).

In particular,
g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}}(\sigma)=\left(u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(u_{{\bf x}^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)d_{{\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf x},q^{\prime},q}(\sigma^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{-1}
.

(3)

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,\gamma\cdot{\bf x}}
are conjugate, then

d_{\gamma\cdot{\bf x},\gamma\cdot{\bf x}^{\prime},\gamma\cdot q,\gamma\cdot q^{\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime})=\rho(\gamma)d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}.

Proof.

(1) Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the representation in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}^{\prime}}
that is conjugate to
\sigma
. Then

\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\xi_{\rho}(x_{2}^{\prime}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(x_{3}^{\prime})\right)=\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x_{2}^{\prime}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{(1)}(x_{3}^{\prime})\right),

so by the definition of
g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}}(\sigma)
,

\displaystyle d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)u_{{\bf x}^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x_{2}^{\prime}),\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{(1)}(x_{3}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)^{-1}\cdot\left(\xi_{\sigma}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\xi_{\sigma}(x_{2}^{\prime}),\xi_{\sigma}^{(1)}(x_{3}^{\prime})\right).

This implies that
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)u_{{\bf x}^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}=u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\sigma}
because
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are conjugate. By definition, for both
m=1,2
,

u_{{\bf x}^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(r_{m})=\xi_{\rho}(r_{m})=u_{{\bf x},q}(\sigma)^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\sigma}(r_{m}),

so the first claim of (1) follows. Since
\xi_{\rho}(r_{1})
and
\xi_{\rho}(r_{2})
are transverse, this first claim implies that
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\sigma)
is diagonalizable, so the second claim of (1) holds.

(2) follows immediately from Lemma
4.1
(1), and (3) is a straight forward consequence of Lemma
4.1
(2) and Lemma
4.1
(3).
∎

4.2.
Defining the tangent cocycle in special cases

Given
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, we will specify the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle associated to
X
, denoted
\nu_{X}\in C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{sl}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
, by specifying its
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
. To do so, we will first define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
when the
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
is in the three special cases labelled as Cases 1 to 3 below. Then, in Section
4.3
, we will use these special cases to define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
for general
h
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
be the lifts to
\widetilde{S}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{J}
respectively, and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
be the barrier system associated to
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. For any
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
, we let
\bar{h}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
denote the
1
-simplex defined by
\bar{h}(t):=h(1-t)
.

Case 1: The endpoints of the image of
h
do not lie in the non-isolated edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.
We will define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
in this case via three steps, each with increasing generality. The first step specifies
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
is the generic situation when the endpoints of the image of
h
lie in the interiors of fragmented ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
. The second and third steps specify
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
in general via an averaging process.

Figure 2.
1
-simplices considered in the first step of Case 1.

For the first step, we define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
when the endpoints of the image of
h
both lie in the interiors of fragmented ideal triangles of the barrier system
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
. For such
h
, let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains
h(0)
, such that
x_{3}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that contains
h(0)
. Similarly, let
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains
h(1)
, such that
y_{3}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that contains
h(1)
, see Figure
2
. Then define

(4.5)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):={\rm d}\left(g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}}^{-1}\right)_{[\rho]}(X)\in\mathfrak{sl}(V),

where
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}
is the map defined by (
4.2
). Note that this is well-defined because switching the roles of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
or switching the roles of
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
leave
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}
invariant. Equivalently, if
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
is a smooth path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
{\rm d}\pi\left(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho_{t}\right)=X
, then

(4.6)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\rho_{t})\in\mathfrak{sl}(V).

Figure 3.
1
-simplices considered in the second step of Case 1.

For the second step, we define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
when both endpoints of the image of
h
lie either in the interiors of fragmented ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, in isolated edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, or in the interiors of non-edge barriers associated to
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. For such
h
, let
h_{1},\dots,h_{4}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices such that

•

h_{1}(0)=h_{2}(0)=:p_{1}
,
h_{3}(0)=h_{4}(0)=:p_{2}
,
h_{1}(1)=h_{3}(1)=:q_{1}
,
h_{2}(1)=h_{4}(1)=:q_{2}
,

•

if
h(0)
lies in the interior of a fragmented ideal triangle, then
p_{1}=p_{2}=h(0)
,

•

if
h(0)
lies in a isolated edge or in the interior of a non-edge barrier, then
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are points in the interiors of the two fragmented ideal triangles that contain
h(0)
in their closures,

•

if
h(1)
lies in the interior of a fragmented ideal triangle, then
q_{1}=q_{2}=h(1)
,

•

if
h(1)
lies in a isolated edge or in the interior of a non-edge barrier, then
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are points in the interiors of the two fragmented ideal triangles that contain
h(1)
in their closures,

see Figure
3
. Note that for all
m=1,\dots,4
, the endpoints of the image of
h_{m}
lie in the interiors of fragmented ideal triangles, so we have already defined
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{m})
in the previous step, see (
4.5
). Thus, we may define

(4.7)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{m=1}^{4}\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{m}).

Observe that
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
does not depend on the choice of
h_{1},\dots,h_{4}
. Also, if
h
satisfies the conditions of the previous step, then the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
given by (
4.7
) agrees with the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
given by (
4.5
).

Figure 4.
1
-simplices considered in Step 3 when
h(0)
and
h(1)
are the centroids of ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.

Finally, for the third step, we define
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
when both endpoints of the image of
h
do not lie in the non-isolated edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. For such
h
, let
h^{1},\dots,h^{9}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices so that

•

h^{1}(0)=h^{2}(0)=h^{3}(0)=:p_{1}
,
h^{4}(0)=h^{5}(0)=h^{6}(0)=:p_{2}
,
h^{7}(0)=h^{8}(0)=h^{9}(0)=:p_{3}
,
h^{1}(1)=h^{4}(1)=h^{7}(1)=:q_{1}
,
h^{2}(1)=h^{5}(1)=h^{8}(1)=:q_{2}
,
h^{3}(1)=h^{6}(1)=h^{9}(1)=:q_{3}
,

•

if
h(0)
lies in the interior of a fragmented ideal triangle, in an isolated edge, or in the interior of a non-edge barrier, then
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}=h(0)
,

•

if
h(0)
is the centroid of an ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p_{3}
are points in the interiors of the three fragmented ideal triangles that contain
h(0)
in its closure,

•

if
h(1)
lies in the interior of a fragmented ideal triangle, in an isolated edge, or in the interior of a non-edge barrier, then
q_{1}=q_{2}=q_{3}=h(1)
,

•

if
h(1)
is the centroid of an ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
are points in the interiors of the three fragmented ideal triangles that contain
h(1)
in its closure,

see Figure
4
. Note that for
m=1,\dots,9
, the endpoints of the image of
h^{m}
both lie in either the interior of fragmented ideal triangles, in an isolated edge, or in the interior of a non-edge barrier, so we have already defined
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{m})
in the previous step. Thus, we can define

(4.8)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=\frac{1}{9}\sum_{m=1}^{9}\widetilde{\nu}(h^{m}).

Again observe that
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
does not depend on the choice of
h^{1},\dots,h^{9}
. Furthermore, if
h
satisfies the conditions of the previous step, then the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
given by (
4.8
) specializes to the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
given by (
4.7
).

The following lemma gives some properties of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}
as defined in (
4.5
).

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
h
and
h^{\prime}
be
1
-simplices satisfying the conditions of Case 1.

(1)

If
h(1)=h^{\prime}(0)
and
h^{\prime\prime}
is the concatenation of
h
and
h^{\prime}
, then
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime\prime})
. In particular,
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)=0
if
h(0)=h(1)
, and
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)=-\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{h})
.

(2)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot h)=\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
, it is sufficient to verify (1) and (2) when the endpoints of the image of
h
and
h^{\prime}
lie in the interiors of fragmented ideal triangles in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, in which case
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime})
are given by (
4.6
). For such
h
and
h^{\prime}
, (1) and (2) are straightforward consequences of Lemma
4.1
(1) and Lemma
4.1
(2) respectively.
∎

Case 2: There is a bridge
J
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across a non-isolated edge
e
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
such that one of the endpoints of the image of
h
is
e\cap J
, while the other is an endpoint of
J
.
First, suppose that
h(1)=e\cap J
and
h(0)
is an endpoint of
J
. Let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains
h(0)
, such that
x_{1}
is an endpoint of
e
and
x_{3}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that contains
h(0)
, see Figure
5
. Similarly, let
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle
T^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains the other endpoint of
J
, such that
x_{1}^{\prime}
is an endpoint of
e
and
x_{3}^{\prime}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that contains the other endpoint of
J
. Let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be the endpoints of
e
that are not
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
respectively. Then define

(4.9)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):={\rm d}\left(u_{{\bf x},q}\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}}^{-1}\right)_{[\rho]}(X)+\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}\left(d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}}^{-1}\right)_{[\rho]}(X)\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V),

where
u_{{\bf x},q}
and
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}
are the maps defined by (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) respectively. Equivalently, if
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
is a smooth path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
{\rm d}\pi\left(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho_{t}\right)=X
, then

(4.10)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\rho_{t})\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V).

Figure 5.
Case 2.

On the other hand, if
h(0)=e\cap J
and
h(1)
is an endpoint of
J
, then the
1
-simplex
\bar{h}
has the property that
\bar{h}(1)=e\cap J
and
\bar{h}(0)
is an endpoint of
J
. Thus,
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{h})
was defined above, so we may define

(4.11)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=-\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{h}).

The following lemma gives the key properties of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
given by (
4.9
) and (
4.11
).

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
h,h^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices satisfying the conditions of Case 2.

(1)

Suppose that there is some bridge
J
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across a non-isolated edge
e
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
such that
h(0)
and
h^{\prime}(1)
are the endpoints of
J
(realized as a geodesic segment in
\widetilde{S}
) and
h(1)=h^{\prime}(0)=e\cap J
. If
h^{\prime\prime}
is the concatenation of
h
and
h^{\prime}
, then
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime\prime})
.

(2)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot h)=\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
.

Proof.

To prove (1), first notice that
h^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the conditions of Case 1. Then (see Figure
5
) by Lemma
4.2
(2),

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)-\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{h}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime\prime}).

(2) follows immediately from Lemma
4.1
(3) and Lemma
4.2
(3).
∎

Case 3: The endpoints of the image of
h
lie in the same non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.
If
h(0)=h(1)
, define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)=0
. If
h(0)\neq h(1)
, let
e=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
be the non-isolated edge that contains both
h(0)
and
h(1)
, and let
\gamma\in\Gamma
be the primitive group element whose axis is
e
, such that
\gamma
translates along
e
in the direction from
h(0)
to
h(1)
. Let
n_{1}(h)
be the number of
\langle\gamma\rangle
-translates of
J
that intersect the interior of
h
, let
n_{2}(h)
be the number of
\langle\gamma\rangle
-translates of
J
that contain the endpoints of
h
, and let

\#h:=n_{1}+\frac{n_{2}}{2}.

Choose any point
r_{3}\in\partial\Gamma
that is not
r_{2}
nor
r_{2}
, let
{\bf r}:=(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})
, and define the map

f_{\bf r}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf r}}\to\PGL(V)

by
f_{\bf r}:\sigma\mapsto\sigma(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
. Note that
f_{\bf r}
is smooth and sends
\rho
to
\id
. Furthermore, the map
f_{\bf r}\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf r}}}^{-1}:\Hit_{V}(S)\to\PGL(V)
depends only on
\gamma
(and not on the choice of
{\bf r}
), so we may define

(4.12)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=\#h\cdot{\rm d}\left(f_{\bf r}\circ\pi|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf r}}}^{-1}\right)_{[\rho]}(X)\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V).

Equivalently, if
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
be a smooth path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf r}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
{\rm d}\pi\left(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho_{t}\right)=X
,
then

(4.13)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=\#h\cdot\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V).

The following lemma follows easily from the definitions.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
h,h^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices satisfying the conditions of Case 3.

(1)

If
h(1)=h^{\prime}(0)
and
h^{\prime\prime}
is the concatenation of
h
and
h^{\prime}
, then
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime\prime})
.

(2)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot h)=\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
.

Finally, we prove the prove the following lemma that relates Cases 1, 2, and 3.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
e
be a non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, let
J
be a bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across
e
, and let
\gamma\in\Gamma
be an element whose axis is
e
. Let
s,s^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices such that
s(1)=s^{\prime}(1)=e\cap J
, and
s(0)
and
s^{\prime}(0)
are the two endpoints of
J
. Let
m,l,l^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices such that
m(0)=s(1)
,
m(1)=\gamma\cdot s(1)
,
l(0)=s(0)
,
l(1)=\gamma\cdot s(0)
,
l^{\prime}(0)=s^{\prime}(0)
,
l^{\prime}(1)=\gamma\cdot s(0)
, see Figure
6
. Then

(1)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(s)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(m)=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot s)
.

(2)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(m)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot\bar{s})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{s})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l)
.

(3)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(m)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot\bar{s})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{s}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l^{\prime})
.

Figure 6.
Lemma
4.6
.

Proof.

First, we prove (1). Let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains
h(0)
, so that
x_{1}
is an endpoint of
e
and
x_{3}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that contains
h(0)
. Similarly, let
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains the other endpoint of
J
, so that
x_{1}^{\prime}
is an endpoint of
e
and
x_{3}^{\prime}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that contains the other endpoint of
J
. Let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be the endpoints of
e
that are not
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
respectively.

Let
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
be a smooth path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
{\rm d}\pi\left(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho_{t}\right)=X
. By (
4.10
),

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(s)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\rho_{t}).

Lemma
4.2
(1) implies that
d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\rho_{t})
commutes with
\rho(\gamma)
for all
t
, so by Lemma
4.4
(2),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\gamma\cdot s)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho(\gamma)u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})\rho(\gamma)^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}d_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}}(\rho_{t}).

Since
\left(\xi_{\rho}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)=\left(\xi_{\rho_{t}}(x_{1}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}(x_{2}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}^{(1)}(x_{3})\right)
for all
t
, it follows that

\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot x_{3})\right)=\left(\xi_{\rho_{t}}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}(\gamma\cdot x_{2}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot x_{3})\right),

so
\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}=g_{{\bf x},\gamma\cdot{\bf x}}(\rho_{t})
. Then by (
4.6
)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}.

Finally, if we set
\rho_{t}^{\prime}:=c_{u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})^{-1}}\circ\rho_{t}
, then
\rho_{t}^{\prime}(\gamma)
fixes both
\xi_{\rho}(x_{1})
and
\xi_{\rho}(q)
. Therefore, by (
4.13
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(m)

\displaystyle=
