\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})^{-1}\rho_{t}(\gamma)u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})\rho(\gamma)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})+\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}+\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho(\gamma)u_{{\bf x},q}(\rho_{t})\rho(\gamma)^{-1}.

It now follows that (1) holds.

(2) follows immediately from (1). Since (2) holds, to prove (3), one only needs to verify that

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{s})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l)=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{s}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l^{\prime}).

By Lemma
4.3
(1) and Lemma
4.4
(1), if
s^{\prime\prime}
is the concatenation of
s^{\prime}
and
\bar{s}
, then

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(s^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\bar{s})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l)=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(s^{\prime\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l)=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(l^{\prime}),

so the required equality holds.
∎

4.3.
Defining the tangent cocycle in general

Next, using Cases 1, 2, and 3 above, we define
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
for general
h
. Given a general
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
, let

h_{--},\,\,h_{-},\,\,\hat{h},\,\,h_{+},\,\,h_{++}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}

be
1
-simplices such that the following holds, see Figure
7
:

•

h(0)=h_{--}(0)
,
h_{--}(1)=h_{-}(0)
,
h_{-}(1)=\hat{h}(0)
,
\hat{h}(1)=h_{+}(0)
,
h_{+}(1)=h_{++}(0)
, and
h_{++}(0)=h(1)
,

•

if
h(0)
does not lie in a non-isolated edge of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then

h(0)=h_{--}(0)=h_{--}(1)=h_{-}(0)=h_{-}(1)=\hat{h}(0),

•

if
h(0)
lies in a non-isolated edge
e\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then

h_{--}(1)=h_{-}(0)=J\cap e
for some bridge
J\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across
e
, and
h_{-}(1)=\hat{h}(0)
is an endpoint of
J
.

•

if
h(1)
does not lie in a non-isolated edge of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then

\hat{h}(1)=h_{+}(0)=h_{+}(1)=h_{++}(0)=h_{++}(0)=h(1).

•

if
h(1)
lies in a non-isolated edge
e\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then

h_{++}(0)=h_{+}(1)=J\cap e
for some bridge
J\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across
e
, and
h_{+}(0)=\hat{h}(1)
is an endpoint of
J
.

Figure 7.
Standard quintuple when
h(0)
and
h(1)
lie in
\mathfrak{D}
.

Definition 4.7
.

For any
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
, we refer to

(h_{--},\,\,h_{-},\,\,\hat{h},\,\,h_{+},\,\,h_{++})

defined above as a
standard quintuple
homotopic to
h
.

Note that each of the five
1
-simplices in a standard quintuple of any
1
-simplex
h
satisfy the conditions of either Case 1, Case 2, or Case 3. Thus, we may define

(4.14)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h):=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{+})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{++}),

Theorem 4.8
.

For any
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
, the map
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
defined above is a well-defined,
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant cocycle.

Proof.

First, we verify that
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}
is well-defined, i.e. we check that

(1)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
as defined by (
4.14
) does not depend on the choice of standard quintuple homotopic to
h
, and

(2)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
as defined by (
4.14
) restricts to the definitions of
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
given by

•

(
4.8
) when
h
satisfies the conditions of Case 1,

•

(
4.9
) or (
4.11
) when
h
satisfies the conditions of Case 2,

•

(
4.12
) when
h
satisfies the conditions of Case 3.

We will only verify (1); once we know that (1) is true, the proof of (2) is a straightforward observation.

To verify (1), pick any two standard quintuples

(h_{--},\,\,h_{-},\,\,\hat{h},\,\,h_{+},\,\,h_{++}),\,\,\text{ and }\,\,(h_{--}^{\prime},\,\,h_{-}^{\prime},\,\,\hat{h}^{\prime},\,\,h_{+}^{\prime},\,\,h_{++}^{\prime})

homotopic to
h
. Let
k:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be a
1
-simplex such that
k(0)=\hat{h}^{\prime}(0)
and
k(1)=\hat{h}(1)
. It suffices to prove that

(4.15)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k)

and

(4.16)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{+}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{++}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{+})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{++}).

Indeed, (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) together imply that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-})+

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{+})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{++})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{+}^{\prime})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{++}^{\prime}).

We will only give the proof of (
4.15
); the proof of (
4.16
) is similar.

Figure 8.
k_{0,1}
,
k_{0,2}
,
k_{1,1}
, and
k_{1,2}
.

If
h(0)
does not lie in a non-isolated edge of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, then the endpoints of the images of
h_{--}
,
h_{-}
,
h_{--}^{\prime}
and
h_{-}^{\prime}
, as well as the backward endpoints of
\hat{h}
and
k
, are all
h(0)
. Hence, Lemma
4.3
(1) implies that

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-}^{\prime})=0\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k),

so (
4.15
) holds. On the other hand, if
h(0)
lies in a non-isolated edge
e
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. Let
k_{1},k_{2}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices such that

•

k_{1}(0)=h_{--}(1)=h_{-}(0)
,

•

k_{1}(1)=h_{--}^{\prime}(1)=h_{-}^{\prime}(0)
,

•

k_{2}(0)=h_{-}(1)=\hat{h}(0)
,

•

k_{2}(1)=h_{-}^{\prime}(1)=\hat{h}^{\prime}(0)=k(0)
,

see Figure
8
. Since the endpoints of
k_{1}
,
h_{--}
and
h_{--}^{\prime}
all lie in the same non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, Lemma
4.5
(1) implies that

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k_{1})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{--}^{\prime}).

Also, Lemma
4.3
(1) implies that

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k_{2})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k)=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(\hat{h})

and parts (2) and (3) of Lemma
4.6
imply that

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k_{1})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{-})+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(k_{2}).

Combining these together proves (
4.15
).

Next, we will prove that
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}
is a cocycle, i.e.

(4.17)

\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)+\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime})=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h^{\prime\prime}).

for all
1
-simplices
h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
such that
h(1)=h^{\prime}(0)
and
h^{\prime\prime}
is the concatenation of
h
with
h^{\prime}
. To do so, choose standard quintuples

(h_{--},\,\,h_{-},\,\,\hat{h},\,\,h_{+},\,\,h_{++}),\,\,(h_{--}^{\prime},\,\,h_{-}^{\prime},\,\,\hat{h}^{\prime},\,\,h_{+}^{\prime},\,\,h_{++}^{\prime}),\,\,(h_{--}^{\prime\prime},\,\,h_{-}^{\prime\prime},\,\,\hat{h}^{\prime\prime},\,\,h_{+}^{\prime\prime},\,\,h_{++}^{\prime\prime})

of
h
,
h^{\prime}
,
h^{\prime\prime}
respectively, such that
h^{\prime\prime}_{--}=h_{--}
,
h^{\prime\prime}_{-}=h_{-}
,
h^{\prime\prime}_{++}=h^{\prime}_{++}
,
h^{\prime\prime}_{+}=h^{\prime}_{+}
,
\bar{h}_{++}=h^{\prime}_{--}
, and
\bar{h}_{+}=h^{\prime}_{-}
. With these choices,
\hat{h}^{\prime\prime}
is a concatenation of
\hat{h}
and
\hat{h}^{\prime}
, so (
4.17
) holds by Lemma
4.3
(1). The
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariance of
\widetilde{\nu}
is a consequence of Lemma
4.3
(2), Lemma
4.4
(2), and Lemma
4.5
(2).
∎

4.4.
Properties of
\Psi

By Theorem
4.8
, we may now define the required linear map

\Psi=\Psi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

by
\Psi(X)=\nu_{X}
. The next proposition proves that
\Psi
satisfies the properties stated at the start of this section.

Proposition 4.9
.

The map
\Psi
is a linear injection and satisfies
[\Psi(X)]=F_{\rho}(X)\in H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{sl}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
for all
X\in T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
. In particular, the set
\mathscr{T}(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
of
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles is a linear subspace of
C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{sl}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
whose dimension is
(2g-2)(n^{2}-1)
.

Proof.

In Section
2.4
, we described the canonical isomorphism

H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\simeq H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho}).

We will verify that
F_{\rho}(X)=[\Psi(X)]
by showing that
F_{\rho}(X)
and
[\Psi(X)]=[\nu_{X}]
are identified via the above isomorphism with the same cohomology class in
H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
. Choose any point
o
that lies in the interior of a fragmented ideal triangle of the barrier system
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, and let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the vertices of the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains
o
, enumerated so that
x_{3}
is not a vertex of the fragmented ideal triangle containing
o
.

First, we describe the group cocycle that represents
[\nu_{X}]
. Let
\widetilde{\nu}_{X}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
be the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of the cocycle
\nu_{X}
. By the description of the isomorphism
H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})\simeq H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
in Section
2.4
, the map

\bar{\nu}_{X}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{sl}(V)

given by
\bar{\nu}_{X}:\gamma\mapsto\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{\gamma})
, where
h_{\gamma}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
is a
1
-simplex with
h_{\gamma}(0)=o
and
h_{\gamma}(1)=\gamma\cdot o
, is a group cocycle that represents
[\nu_{X}]
.

Next, we describe the group cocycle that represents
F_{\rho}(X)
. Let
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
be a smooth path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
, and

{\rm d}\pi\left(\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho_{t}\right)=X.

Then by the description of the isomorphism

T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\simeq H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

given in Section
2.4
, the map
\mu_{X}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{sl}(V)
given by
\mu_{X}:\gamma\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
, is a group cocycle that represents
F_{\rho}(X)
.

To prove
F_{\rho}(X)=[\Psi(X)]
, it now suffices to show that
\mu_{X}=\bar{\nu}_{X}
. To see this, observe that

\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\left(\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot x_{2}),\xi_{\rho}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot x_{3})\right)=\left(\xi_{\rho_{t}}(\gamma\cdot x_{1}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}(\gamma\cdot x_{2}),\xi_{\rho_{t}}^{(1)}(\gamma\cdot x_{3}))\right),

so
\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}=g_{{\bf x},\gamma\cdot{\bf x}}(\rho_{t})
. Thus,

\bar{\nu}_{X}(\gamma)=\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h_{\gamma})=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},\gamma\cdot{\bf x}}(\rho_{t})=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}=\mu_{X}(\gamma).

Next, we prove that
\Psi
is an injective linear map. To check that
\Psi
is injective, let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be vectors in
T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
. If
\nu_{X_{1}}=\nu_{X_{2}}
, then we have proven that
F_{\rho}(X_{1})=[\nu_{X_{1}}]=[\nu_{X_{2}}]=F_{\rho}(X_{2})
, which means that
X_{1}=X_{2}
because
F_{\rho}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to H^{1}(S,\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})
is an isomorphism. Thus,
\Psi
is injective. That fact that
\Psi
is linear follows immediately from the observation that when
h
satisfies the conditions of Cases 1, 2, or 3 in Section
4.2
, the map

T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)

given by
X\mapsto\widetilde{\nu}_{X}(h)
(see (
4.5
), (
4.9
), and (
4.12
)), is linear.
∎

5.
The
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization of
T\Hit_{V}(S)

Fix an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
on
S
and a compatible bridge system
\mathcal{J}
. Using the pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
, Bonahon and Dreyer
[
BD14
]
gave an explicit, real-analytic parameterization of
\Hit_{V}(S)
by an open convex cone
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{T}}
in a
(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)
-dimensional subspace
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{W}_{\mathcal{T}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{T}^{o}\times\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{B}}
, where
\mathcal{A}
denotes the set of pairs of positive integers that sum to
n
, and
\mathcal{B}
denotes the set of triples of positive integers that sum to
n
. (Recall that
\mathcal{T}^{o}
is the oriented ideal triangulation associated to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{M}
is the set of non-edge barriers in the barrier system
\mathcal{D}
associated to
\mathcal{T}
.) In the companion paper
[
SWZ20
]
, we defined a (
n^{2}-1)(2g-2)
-dimensional family of pairwise commuting vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, called the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields
. We further showed that these can be described easily using a reparamaterization of Bonahon and Dreyer’s parameterization of
\Hit_{V}(S)
by
\mathscr{C}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

[
SWZ20
, Theorem 1.1]

There is a real-analytic diffeomorphism

\Omega=\Omega_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathscr{C}

such that the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
are exactly the vector fields that are identified via

{\rm d}\Omega:T\Hit_{V}(S)\to T\mathscr{C}\cong\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{W}

to the constant vector fields on
\mathscr{C}
.

The trivialization
{\rm d}\Omega
is called the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization
of
T\Hit_{V}(S)
.

The goal of this section is to relate the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization to the tangent cocycles described in Section
2.4
. More precisely, fix
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
and a representative
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
. Let
\xi_{\rho}:\partial\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}(V)
denote the
\rho
-equivariant Frenet curve. Recall that in Section
4
, we defined a linear embedding

\Psi=\Psi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to C^{1}(S,\mathfrak{sl}(V)_{\Ad\circ\rho})

whose image
\mathscr{T}=\mathscr{T}(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
is the space of
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles. We will explicitly define a linear map

\Xi=\Xi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:\mathscr{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{T}^{o}\times\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{B}}

so that the following theorem holds.

Theorem 5.2
.

The map
\Xi
is a linear embedding whose image is the subspace
\mathscr{W}
. Furthermore,

{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}=\Xi\circ\Psi:T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)\to T_{\Omega([\rho])}\mathscr{C}\cong\mathscr{W}.

Theorem
5.2
will later be used in Section
6
to show that the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization is symplectic.

The linear map
\Xi
will be defined in several steps. Let
\nu
be a
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle, and let
\widetilde{\nu}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{sl}(V))
be its
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift. First, in Section
5.1
, we introduce the notion of an
eruption endomorphism
, and use it to specify the set of possible values of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
when
h
is a
1
-simplex that crosses exactly one non-edge barrier in the barrier system
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
associated to
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. Then, in Section
5.2
, we introduce the notion of a
shearing endomorphism
, and use it to specify the set of possible values of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
when
h
is
1
-simplex that crosses exactly one isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, and also the set of possible values of the “diagonal part" of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
when
h
is a
1
-simplex whose image is a bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
. Using these, we define
\Xi
in Section
5.3
and prove the first statement in Theorem
5.2
, i.e. that
\Xi
is injective and its image
\mathscr{W}
. Finally, in Section
5.4
, we recall the description of the map
\Omega
, and combine the definition of
\Xi
with results from the companion paper
[
SWZ20
]
to prove the second statement of Theorem
5.2
.

5.1.
Eruption endomorphisms

Given any non-edge barrier
\mathbf{b}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, let
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
, and
x_{3}
be the vertices of the ideal triangle
T
containing
{\bf b}
so that
x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<x_{1}
and
x_{1}
is the terminal endpoint of
{\bf b}
. We say that
{\bf x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is the
triple associated to
{\bf b}
, see Figure
9
. Let
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
(resp.
\mathfrak{T}_{3}
) be the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
in
T
that does not have
x_{2}
(resp.
x_{3}
) as a vertex. We say that a
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}

passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}
if
h(0)
and
h(1)
lie in the interiors of
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{3}
respectively, see Figure
9
.

Figure 9.
h
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}
.

Let
\nu
be a
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle, and let
\widetilde{\nu}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{sl}(V))
be its
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift. We will now describe the possible values of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
, where
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
. To do so, we use the notion of an eruption endomorphism.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
be a triple of positive integers that sum to
n
, and let
\mathbf{F}=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
be a generic triple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
. The
\mathbf{i}
-eruption endomorphism with respect to
\mathbf{F}
is the endomorphism
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
with eigenspaces
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}
and
F_{2}^{(i_{2})}+F_{3}^{(i_{3})}
corresponding to eigenvalues
\frac{i_{2}+i_{3}}{n}
and
-\frac{i_{1}}{n}
respectively.

For any triple
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, and any generic triple of flags
\mathbf{F}=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
in
\mathcal{F}(V)
, observe that

A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}+A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}+A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\mathbf{F}_{-}}=0,

where
\mathbf{i}_{+}:=(i_{2},i_{3},i_{1})
,
\mathbf{i}_{-}:=(i_{3},i_{1},i_{2})
,
\mathbf{F}_{+}:=(F_{2},F_{3},F_{1})
, and
\mathbf{F}_{-}:=(F_{3},F_{1},F_{2})
. Also, if

\{f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,n}\}

is a basis of
V
such that
F_{m}^{(j)}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,j}\}
for all
m=1,2,3
and
j=1,\dots,n
, then the genericity of
\mathbf{F}
implies that

(5.1)

\displaystyle\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}\}

is a basis of
V
. In this basis, we can write
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
as the matrix

A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{i_{2}+i_{3}}{n}\cdot\id_{i_{1}}&0\\
0&-\frac{i_{1}}{n}\cdot\id_{i_{2}+i_{3}}\end{array}\right],

where
\id_{j}
is the
j\times j
identity matrix.

The next proposition states some important properties of
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathbf{F}:=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
be a generic triple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
.

(1)

For all triples
{\bf i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of positive integers that sum to
n
,
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot F_{1}^{(j)}\subset F_{1}^{(j)}
for all
j=1,\dots,n-1
, but
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}\not\subset F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}
and
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot F_{3}^{(i_{3}+1)}\not\subset F_{3}^{(i_{3}+1)}
.

(2)

Let
G_{\mathbf{F}}\subset\PGL(V)
be the Lie subgroup defined by

G_{\mathbf{F}}:=\{g\in\PGL(V):g\cdot F_{1}=F_{1},\,\,g\cdot F_{2}^{(1)}=F_{2}^{(1)},\,\,\text{ and }\,\,g\cdot F_{3}^{(1)}=F_{3}^{(1)}\}.

Then the set
\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}
is a basis for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{F}}
of
G_{\mathbf{F}}
.

Proof.

We first compute
A_{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathbf{i}}\cdot v
for any vector
v\in V
. Observe that we may write
v
uniquely as

v=\sum_{j=1}^{i_{1}}a_{1,j}f_{1,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{i_{2}}a_{2,j}f_{2,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{i_{3}}a_{3,j}f_{3,j}

for some constants
a_{m,j}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}\}
is the basis given by (
5.1
). Then

(5.2)

\displaystyle A_{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathbf{i}}\cdot v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{i_{1}}\frac{i_{2}+i_{3}}{n}a_{1,j}f_{1,j}-\sum_{j=1}^{i_{2}}\frac{i_{1}}{n}a_{2,j}f_{2,j}-\sum_{j=1}^{i_{3}}\frac{i_{1}}{n}a_{3,j}f_{3,j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{i_{1}}a_{1,j}f_{1,j}-\frac{i_{1}}{n}v.

First, we prove (1). It follows immediately from (
5.2
) implies that
A^{\bf i}_{\bf F}\cdot F_{1}^{(j)}\subset F_{1}^{(j)}
. On the other hand, if
v\in F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}\setminus F_{2}^{(i_{2})}
, then the assumption that
{\bf F}
is generic implies that
a_{1,i_{1}}\neq 0
, and that
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}
is transverse to
F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}
. Thus, by (
5.2
) that
A_{\mathbf{F}}^{\mathbf{i}}\cdot v\notin F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}
, which implies that
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}\not\subset F_{2}^{(i_{2}+1)}
. Similarly,
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot F_{3}^{(i_{3}+1)}\not\subset F_{3}^{(i_{3}+1)}
.

To prove (2), first observe that
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{F}}
:
Indeed, it follows from the definition of
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
that
\exp(tA^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}})
fixes
F_{2}^{(1)}
and
F_{3}^{(1)}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and (1) implies that
\exp(tA^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}})
fixes
F_{1}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. It is also a straightforward calculation using the genericity of
\mathbf{F}
that the dimension of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{F}}
is also
\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that the dimension of
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}
is
\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}
, i.e.
\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}
is a linearly independent collection of endomorphisms.

Suppose for contradiction that there is a non-empty collection
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of triples of positive integers that sum to
n
, such that

(5.3)

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\alpha_{\mathbf{i}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}=0

and
\alpha_{\mathbf{i}}\in\mathbb{R}
are non-zero for all
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Let
k:=\min\{i_{2}:\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\}
, let

\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}:=\{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:i_{2}=k\},

and let
\mathbf{j}:=(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
be the triple whose first coordinate
j_{1}
is the largest among all triples
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
. By the definition of
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
,

A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}=-\frac{i_{1}}{n}f_{2,k+1}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{2,k+1}\}

for all
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
. Also, (
5.2
) implies that

A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}\in F_{1}^{(i_{1})}+\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{2,k+1}\}\subset F_{1}^{(j_{1}-1)}+\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{2,k+1}\}

for all
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{\mathbf{j}\}
. As a consequence, we see that

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\setminus\{\mathbf{j}\}}\alpha_{\mathbf{i}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}\in F_{1}^{(j_{1}-1)}+\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{2,k+1}\}.

On the other hand, if we write

f_{2,k+1}=f_{2,j_{2}+1}=\sum_{j=1}^{j_{1}}a_{1,j}f_{1,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{2}}a_{2,j}f_{2,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{3}}a_{3,j}f_{3,j}

for some
a_{m,j}\in\mathbb{R}
, then the genericity of
\mathbf{F}
implies that
a_{1,j_{1}}\neq 0
. It then follows from (
5.2
) that
A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}
lies in
F_{1}^{(j_{1})}+\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{2,k+1}\}
, but not in
F_{1}^{(j_{1}-1)}+\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\{f_{2,k+1}\}
. Thus,

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\alpha_{\mathbf{i}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\setminus\{\mathbf{j}\}}\alpha_{\mathbf{i}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}+A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot f_{2,k+1}\neq 0,

which contradicts (
5.3
).
∎

We now relate eruption endomorphisms to
\widetilde{\nu}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
{\bf b}
be a non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the triple associated to
{\bf b}
, and let
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be a
1
-simplex that passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}
. Then
for each triple
{\bf i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, there is a unique
\alpha^{{\bf i}}_{{\bf b}}=\alpha^{{\bf i}}_{{\bf b}}(\nu)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{\nu}(h)=\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}.

Furthermore,

(1)

if
{\bf i}_{+}:=(i_{2},i_{3},i_{1})
,
{\bf i}_{-}:=(i_{3},i_{1},i_{2})
, and
{\bf b}_{+}
(resp.
{\bf b}_{-}
) is the non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
with the same source as
{\bf b}
but whose terminal endpoint is
x_{2}
(resp.
x_{3}
), see Figure
9
, then

\alpha^{{\bf i}}_{{\bf b}}=\alpha^{{\bf i}_{+}}_{{\bf b}_{+}}=\alpha^{{\bf i}_{-}}_{{\bf b}_{-}}.

(2)

if
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then

\alpha^{{\bf i}}_{{\bf b}}=\alpha^{{\bf i}}_{\gamma\cdot{\bf b}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
be the real-analytic submanifold of
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
defined by (
4.1
), let
{\bf x}_{-}:=(x_{3},x_{1},x_{2})
and
{\bf x}_{+}:=(x_{2},x_{3},x_{1})
, and let
g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}_{-}}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}\to\PGL(V)
be the real-analytic map defined by (
4.2
).
Observe from the definitions that
g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}_{-}}(\sigma)\in G_{\xi_{\rho}(\bf x)}
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}_{-}}(\rho)=\id
. By (
4.6
) and Lemma
4.3
(1),
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=-\widetilde{\nu}(\bar{h})=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}_{-}}(\rho_{t})
for some smooth path
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
with
\rho_{0}=\rho
. Proposition
5.4
(2) then implies the first claim of the proposition.

To prove (1), choose a basis
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
such that
f_{j}\in\xi_{\rho}^{(j)}(x_{2})\cap\xi_{\rho}^{(n+1-j)}(x_{3})
for all
j=1,\dots,n
. For any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
, Proposition
5.4
(1) implies that
A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}
and
A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}
are given by an upper triangular and lower triangular matrix in this basis respectively. Furthermore, neither
A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}
nor
A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}
is given by a diagonal matrix in this basis. This, together with Proposition
5.4
(2), imply that

\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}\cup\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}

is a linearly independent collection of endomorphisms in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
.

Now, suppose for the purpose of contradiction that
\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}\neq\alpha^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\mathbf{b}_{+}}
or
\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}\neq\alpha^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\mathbf{b}_{-}}
for some
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
. Let
h,h_{+},h_{-}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices that pass from the left to the right of
{\bf b}
,
{\bf b}_{+}
, and
{\bf b}_{-}
respectively,
h(1)=h_{+}(0)
,
h_{+}(1)=h_{-}(0)
, and
h_{-}(1)=h(0)
, see Figure
9
. Then Proposition
5.5
implies

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(h)+\widetilde{\nu}(h_{+})+\widetilde{\nu}(h_{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\left(\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}+\alpha^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\mathbf{b}_{+}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}+\alpha^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\mathbf{b}_{-}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\left((\alpha^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\mathbf{b}_{+}}-\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}+(\alpha^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\mathbf{b}_{-}}-\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}})\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}\right)

where the third equality is the observation that
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}+A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}+A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}=0
. This is impossible because

\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}\cup\left\{A^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}:i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}=n\right\}

is a linearly independent collection.

(2) is an immediate consequence of the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariance of
\widetilde{\nu}
, and the observation that

\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}=A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot\mathbf{x})}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
∎

By Proposition
5.5
(2), for all triples of positive integers
{\bf i}
that sum to
n
and all non-edge barriers
\widehat{\bf b}
in
\mathcal{D}
, we may define

(5.4)

\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}(\nu):=\alpha^{\bf i}_{\bf b}(\nu)

where
{\bf b}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
is some (equiv. any) lift of
\widehat{\bf b}
to
\widetilde{S}
.

5.2.
Shearing endomorphisms

Given any oriented edge
{\bf e}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
, let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be the backward and forward endpoints of
{\bf e}
. We say that
\mathbf{r}:=(r_{1},r_{2})
is the
pair associated to
{\bf e}
. If
{\bf e}
is isolated, let
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
(resp.
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
) be the fragmented ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that has
{\bf e}
as an edge and lies to the left (resp. right) of
{\bf e}
. We say that a
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}

passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
if
h(0)
and
h(1)
lie in the interiors of
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
respectively, see Figure
10
. On the other hand, if
{\bf e}
is non-isolated and
J
is a bridge across
{\bf e}
, we say that a
1
-simplex
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}

passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
along
J
if
h(0)
and
h(1)
are the endpoints of
J
that lie to the left and right of
{\bf e}
respectively.

Figure 10.
h
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
when
{\bf e}
is isolated (on the left) and when
{\bf e}
is not isolated (on the right).

Again, let
\nu
be a
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle, and let
\widetilde{\nu}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{sl}(V))
be its
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift. We will now describe the possible values of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
when
h
passes from the left to the right of an isolated edge
{\bf e}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
, and the possible values of the “diagonal part" of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
with respect to
\xi_{\rho}({\bf r})
when
h
passes from the left to the right of an isolated edge
{\bf e}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
along a bridge
J
across
{\bf e}
. For that purpose, we define the shearing endomorphisms.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\mathbf{k}:=(k_{1},k_{2})
be a pair of positive integers that sum to
n
and let
\mathbf{E}:=(E_{1},E_{2})
be a generic pair of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
.
The
\mathbf{k}
-
shearing endomorphism
with respect to
\mathbf{E}
is the endomorphism
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
with eigenspaces
E_{1}^{(k_{1})}
and
E_{2}^{(k_{2})}
corresponding to eigenvalues
\frac{k_{2}}{2n}
and
-\frac{k_{1}}{2n}
respectively.

For any pair
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
of positive integers that sum to
n
and any generic pair of flags
(E_{1},E_{2})
in
\mathcal{F}(V)
, observe that

D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}=-D^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\bar{\mathbf{E}}},

where
\bar{\mathbf{k}}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
and
\bar{\mathbf{E}}:=(E_{2},E_{1})
. Also, if

\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}

is a basis for
V
such that
f_{j}\in E_{1}^{(j)}\cap E_{2}^{(n+1-j)}
for all
j=1,\dots,n
, then in this basis, the endomorphism
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}
is written as the matrix

D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{k_{2}}{2n}\cdot\id_{k_{1}}&0\\
0&-\frac{k_{1}}{2n}\cdot\id_{k_{2}}\end{array}\right].

Furthermore, it is clear that the following proposition holds.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\mathbf{E}:=(E_{1},E_{2})
be a generic pair of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
. Then

(1)

For all pairs
\mathbf{k}:=(k_{1},k_{2})
of positive integers that sum to
n
,
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}
fixes both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
.

(2)

Let
G_{\mathbf{E}}\subset\PGL(V)
be the subgroup defined by

G_{\mathbf{E}}:=\{g\in\PGL(V):g\cdot E_{1}=E_{1}\text{ and }g\cdot E_{2}=E_{2}\}.

Then
\left\{D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}:k_{1}+k_{2}=n\right\}
is a basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{E}
of
G_{E}
.

The next pair of propositions relate shearing endomorphisms to
\widetilde{\nu}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
{\bf e}
be an oriented, isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
, let
\mathbf{r}:=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, and let
h
be a
1
-simplex that passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
. Then for each pair
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, there is a unique
\alpha^{{\bf k}}_{{\bf e}}=\alpha^{{\bf k}}_{{\bf e}}(\nu)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{\nu}(h)=-\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=n}2\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}.

Furthermore,

(1)

if
\bar{\bf k}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
and
\bar{\bf e}
is the edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
whose backward and forward endpoints are
r_{2}
and
r_{1}
respectively, then

\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}=\alpha^{\bar{\bf k}}_{\bar{\bf e}}.

(2)

if
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then
\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}=\alpha^{\bf k}_{\gamma\cdot\bf e}
.

Proof.

For both
m=1,2
, let
q_{m}
be the vertex of the ideal triangle containing
\mathfrak{T}_{m}
that is neither
r_{1}
nor
r_{2}
. Let
{\bf x}:=(r_{2},r_{1},q_{1})
, let
{\bf y}:=(r_{1},r_{2},q_{2})
, let
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
be the real-analytic submanifold of
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)
defined by (
4.1
), and let
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}:\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}\to\PGL(V)
be the real-analytic map defined by (
4.2
).
Observe from the definitions that
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\sigma)\in G_{\bf E}
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
and
g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\rho)=\id
. By (
4.6
),
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\rho_{t})
for some smooth path
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
with
\rho_{0}=\rho
, so the first claim follows from Proposition
5.7
(2).

(1) follows from the observations that
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=-\widetilde{\nu}(\bar{h})
and
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=-D^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\bar{\mathbf{r}})}
for all
{\bf k}
, where
\bar{\bf r}:=(r_{2},r_{1})
, while (2) is an immediate consequence of the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariance of
\widetilde{\nu}
, and the observation that

\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot\mathbf{r})}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
∎

To state the next proposition, we need to define formally specify the “diagonal part" of endomorphisms, we observe the following. Let
{\bf r}:=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to an oriented, non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
. If
M\in\mathfrak{sl}(V)
is an endomorphism for which there is some
m=1,2
such that

M\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{m})\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{m})

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, then we may decompose

(5.5)

M=M_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+M_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

where
M_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{1})\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{1})
and
M_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{2})\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{2})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, while
M_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
is nilpotent and satisfies
M_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{m})\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{m})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. We refer to
M_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
(resp.
M_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
) as the
diagonal part (resp. nilpotent part) of
M
with respect to
{\bf r}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
{\bf e}
be an oriented, non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
, let
\mathbf{r}:=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, let
J
be a bridge across
{\bf e}
, and let
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be a
1
-simplex such that
h(0)
is the endpoint of
J
that lies to the left of
{\bf e}
and
h(1)
is the intersection of
{\bf e}
and
J
. If
\widetilde{\nu}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
denotes the diagonal part of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
with respect to
\xi_{\rho}({\bf r})
, see (
5.5
), then for each pair
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
of positive integers that sum to
n
, there is a unique
\alpha^{{\bf k}}_{{\bf e}}=\alpha^{{\bf k}}_{{\bf e}}(\nu)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\widetilde{\nu}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=-\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=n}\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}.

Furthermore,

(1)

if
\bar{\bf k}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
and
\bar{\bf e}
is the edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
whose backward and forward endpoints are
r_{2}
and
r_{1}
respectively, then

\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}=\alpha^{\bar{\bf k}}_{\bar{\bf e}}.

(2)

if
\gamma\in\Gamma
, then
\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}=\alpha^{\bf k}_{\gamma\cdot\bf e}
.

Proof.

The first claim is a consequence of the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
and Proposition
5.7
(2). To prove (1), let
h^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be a
1
-simplex such that
h^{\prime}(0)
is the endpoint of
J
that lies to the left of
\bar{\bf e}
and
h^{\prime}(1)=h(1)
. By Lemma
4.2
(2) and the definition of
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
and
\widetilde{\nu}(h^{\prime})
, see (
4.9
),
\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=-\widetilde{\nu}(h^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
. Thus

\displaystyle-\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=n}\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=-\widetilde{\nu}(h^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=-\widetilde{\nu}(h^{\prime})_{\bar{\bf r},{\rm diag}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=n}\alpha^{\bar{\bf k}}_{\bar{\bf e}}\cdot D^{\bar{\bf k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\bar{\bf r})}=-\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=n}\alpha^{\bar{\bf k}}_{\bar{\bf e}}\cdot D^{{\bf k}}_{\xi_{\rho}({\bf r})}

(1) now follows from Proposition
5.7
(2). (2) is an immediate consequence of the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariance of
\widetilde{\nu}
, and the observation that

\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\gamma\cdot\mathbf{r})}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
∎

By Proposition
5.8
(2) and Proposition
5.9
(2), for all pairs of positive integers
{\bf k}
that sum to
n
and all oriented edges
\widehat{\bf e}
in
\mathcal{T}^{o}
, we may define

(5.6)

\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}(\nu):=\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}(\nu).

where
{\bf e}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
is some (equiv. any) lift
\widehat{\bf e}
of to
\widetilde{S}
.

5.3.
The linear map
\Xi

We may now define the required linear map

(5.7)

\displaystyle\Xi=\Xi_{\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:\mathscr{T}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{T}^{o}\times\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{B}},

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\left(\left(\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}(\nu)\right)_{(\widehat{\bf e},{\bf k})\in\mathcal{T}^{o}\times\mathcal{A}},\left(\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}(\nu)\right)_{(\widehat{\bf b},{\bf i})\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{B}}\right),

where
\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}(\nu)
and
\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}(\nu)
are the real numbers defined by (
5.4
) and (
5.6
) respectively. To describe the image of
\Xi
, we use the following notation.

Notation 5.10
.

•

For every oriented, non-isolated edge
{\bf e}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}

across
{\bf e}

and respectively lie to the left and right of

that fixes both

and

) as its repelling fixed point.

•

–

(resp.

(resp.

For each integer

and

(resp.

and

(resp.

•

For every non-isolated edge
\widehat{\bf e}
of
\mathcal{T}^{o}
, let
{\bf e}
be a oriented, non-isolated edge of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that projects to
\widehat{\bf e}
and let

be a bridge across
{\bf e}
. Then let
H(\widehat{\bf e}):=H({\bf e})
, let
H^{\prime}(\widehat{\bf e}):=H^{\prime}({\bf e})
, and for each integer
j\in\{1,\dots,H({\bf e})\}
(resp.
j\in\{1,\dots,H^{\prime}({\bf e})\}
), let
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
and
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
(resp.
\widehat{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
) be the projections in
\mathcal{D}
of
{\bf e}_{j}({\bf e},J)
and
{\bf b}_{j}({\bf e},J)
(resp.
{\bf e}_{j}^{\prime}({\bf e},J)
and
{\bf b}_{j}^{\prime}({\bf e},J)
) respectively. Observe that
H(\widehat{\bf e})
,
H^{\prime}(\widehat{\bf e})
,
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
,
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
,
\widehat{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
) do not depend on the choice of
{\bf e}
and
J
.

•

For each integer
j
, let
l_{j}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
(resp.
l_{j}^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
) be a
1
-simplex such that
l_{j}(0)
and
l_{j}(1)
(resp.
l_{j}^{\prime}(0)
and
l_{j}^{\prime}(1)
) lie in the interiors of
\mathfrak{T}_{j,-}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{j,+}
(resp.
\mathfrak{T}_{j,-}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{j,+}^{\prime}
) respectively. Then let
m_{j}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
(resp.
m_{j}^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
) be a
1
-simplex such that
m_{j}(0)=l_{j}(1)
and
m_{j}(1)=l_{j+1}(0)
(resp.
m_{j}^{\prime}(0)=l_{j}^{\prime}(1)
and
m_{j}^{\prime}(1)=l_{j+1}^{\prime}(0)
).

•

Let
s,s^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be
1
-simplices such that
s(0)
is the endpoint of
J
that lies in
T
,
s^{\prime}(0)
is the endpoint of
J
that lies in
T^{\prime}
,
s(1)=s^{\prime}(1)=e\cap J=m(0)=m^{\prime}(0)
,
m(1)=\gamma\cdot m(0)
, and
m^{\prime}(1)=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime}(0)
.

Figure 11.
In both diagrams above, the non-isolated edge
e
with endpoints
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
is drawn in thick red, and the
\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle=\langle\gamma_{2}\rangle
orbit of the bridge
J
is drawn in dotted green. On the left,
H=3
and
H^{\prime}=2
. On the right,
H=4
and
H^{\prime}=3
.

Since the images of
m
and
m^{\prime}
lie in
{\bf e}
,
\widetilde{\nu}(m)\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{k})\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{k})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(m^{\prime})\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{k})\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(r_{k})
for both
k=1,2
and all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, see (
4.12
).
Also, by definition, if
h=s
,
l_{j}
or
m_{j}
for some
j
, then

\widetilde{\nu}(h)\cdot\xi^{(i)}(x)\subset\xi^{(i)}(x)

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. In particular,
we may decompose

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(s)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

see (
5.5
). Similarly, we may decompose

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}(s^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(s^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}.

Proposition 5.11
.

(1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(m)=\sum_{j=1}^{H}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
.

(2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
.

Proof.

Let
l:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be the concatenation
m_{1}\cdot l_{1}\cdot m_{2}\cdot l_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot m_{H}\cdot l_{H}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
l(0)=s(0)
and
l(1)=\gamma\cdot l(0)
. Then by the finite additivity of
\widetilde{\nu}
,

(5.8)

\widetilde{\nu}(l)=\sum_{j=1}^{H}\left(\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{H}\left(\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\right).

At the same time, by Lemma
4.6
(1),

(5.9)

\widetilde{\nu}(l)=\widetilde{\nu}(m)+\widetilde{\nu}(s)-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma\cdot s)=\widetilde{\nu}(m)+\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

where the second equality holds because the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariance of
\widetilde{\nu}
and the fact that
\gamma
fixes
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
imply

\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma)\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}.

By comparing the diagonal parts of (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) we see that (1) holds.

Next, we prove (2). By Corollary
2.8
,
\rho(\gamma)
has
\xi_{\rho}(x)
as a repelling fixed flag, and

0<K:=\max\left\{\frac{\lambda_{a}(\rho(\gamma))}{\lambda_{b}(\rho(\gamma))}:a>b\right\}<1,

where
\lambda_{1}(\rho(\gamma))>\dots>\lambda_{n}(\rho(\gamma))
are the eigenvalues of some (any) linear representative of
\rho(\gamma)
. Observe that for all positive integers
t
,

\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma^{t})(\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}})=\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t}\cdot l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}=\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j+tH})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}.

Similarly,

\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma^{t})(\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}})=\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j+tH})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma^{t})(\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}})=\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}.

Since the nilpotent endomorphisms
\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
,
\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
, and
\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
are all represented by upper triangular matrices if
x=r_{1}
, and lower triangular matrices if
x=r_{2}
, so it follows that

\|\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j+tH})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\|\leq K^{t}\|\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\|,\,\,\|\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j+tH})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\|\leq K^{t}\|\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\|,

and

\|\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t+1}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\|\leq K^{t}\|\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\|

for all positive integers
t
, where
\|\cdot\|
is the Euclidean norm on the space of
n\times n
matrices. In particular,
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
and
\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t+1}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
both converge absolutely. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma^{t})\cdot\left(\sum_{j=1}^{H}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\Ad\circ\rho(\gamma^{t})\cdot\left(\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}-\widetilde{\nu}(\gamma^{t+1}\cdot s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}=\widetilde{\nu}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}},

where the second equality comes from comparing the nilpotent parts of (
5.8
) and (
5.9
). This proves (2).
∎

Notation 5.12
.

Let
\pi_{S}:\widetilde{S}\to S
be the covering map.

(1)

Given an oriented edge
\widehat{\bf e}
in
\mathcal{T}^{o}
, let
{\bf e}
be an oriented edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
such that
\pi_{S}({\bf e})=\widehat{\bf e}
, and denote
\widehat{\bar{\bf e}}:=\pi_{S}(\bar{\bf e})
.

(2)

Given a non-edge barrier
\widehat{\bf b}
of
\mathcal{M}
, let
{\bf b}
be a non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\pi_{S}({\bf b})=\widehat{\bf b}
, and let
\widehat{\bf b}_{+}:=\pi_{S}({\bf b}_{+})
and
\widehat{\bf b}_{-}:=\pi_{S}({\bf b}_{-})
.

Recall that for every
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
, we denote
\bar{\bf k}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
, and for every
{\bf i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}
, we denote
{\bf i}_{+}:=(i_{2},i_{3},i_{1})
and
{\bf i}_{-}:=(i_{3},i_{1},i_{2})
. Also, let
\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}:i_{1}=k\}
for every
k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Then let

\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{W}_{\mathcal{T}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{T}^{o}\times\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{B}}

denote the subspace consisting of all the vectors

\alpha=\left(\left(\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}\right)_{(\widehat{\bf e},{\bf k})\in\mathcal{T}^{o}\times\mathcal{A}},\left(\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}\right)_{(\widehat{\bf b},{\bf i})\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{B}}\right)

that satisfy the following linear conditions:

(I)

(Symmetry for triples) For each
\widehat{\bf b}\in\mathcal{M}
and each
{\bf i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}
,

\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}=\alpha^{\bf i_{+}}_{\widehat{\bf b}_{+}}=\alpha^{\bf i_{-}}_{\widehat{\bf b}_{-}},

(II)

(Symmetry for pairs) For each
\widehat{\bf e}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}
and each
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
,

\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}=\alpha^{\bar{\bf k}}_{\widehat{\bar{\bf e}}}.

(III)

(Closed leaf equalities) For each non-isolated edge
\widehat{\bf e}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}
and each
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
,

\sum_{j=1}^{H}\left(\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\left(\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}_{j}}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{2}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{j}}\right)&\text{if }x\neq x^{\prime};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\left(\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}_{j}}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{j}}\right)&\text{if }x=x^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

where
H=H(\widehat{\bf e})
,
H^{\prime}=H^{\prime}(\widehat{\bf e})
,
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}=\widehat{\bf e}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
,
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}=\widehat{\bf b}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
,
\widehat{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}=\widehat{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}=\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}(\widehat{\bf e})
.

It is a result of Bonahon and Dreyer
[
BD14
, Section 4]
that

\dim\mathscr{W}=(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)=\dim\Hit_{V}(S)

(the equalities specified in (III) above are called the
closed leaf equalities
in
[
BD14
]
).

Proposition 5.13
.

\Xi
is a linear embedding whose image is
\mathscr{W}
.

Proof.

The linearity of
\Xi
is immediate from its definition. We first prove that
\Xi
is injective. Suppose that
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles such that
\Xi(\nu_{1})=\Xi(\nu_{2})
. For both
m=1,2
, let
\widetilde{\nu}_{m}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{sl}(V)
denote the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of
\nu_{m}
. Observe that every
1
-simplex in
\widetilde{S}
is homotopic to the concatenation of finitely many
1
-simplices, each of which satisfies one of the following:

(i)

its image intersects exactly one barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, which is either an isolated edge or a non-edge barrier,

(ii)

there is a bridge
J
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across a non-isolated edge
e
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
such that one of the endpoints of its image is
e\cap J
and the other is an endpoint of
J
,

(iii)

the endpoints of its image lie in the same non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.

To prove that
\Xi
is injective, it thus suffices to verify that
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(h)=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}(h)
for all
1
-simplices
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
that satisfies (i), (ii), or (iii).

First suppose that
h
satisfies (i). Then
h
satisfies Case 1 in Section
4.2
, so by definition, it suffices to only consider the situation where both endpoints of
h
do not lie in the barrier that
h
intersects. If the barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that
h
intersects is a non-edge barrier
{\bf b}
, let
{\bf x}
be the triple associated to
{\bf b}
. Then either
h
or
\bar{h}
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}
, so by definition (see Proposition
5.5
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(h)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\bf i}_{\bf b}(\nu_{1})\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}&\text{if }h\text{ passes from the left to the right of }{\bf b};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\bf i}_{\bf b}(\nu_{1})\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}&\text{if }\bar{h}\text{ passes from the left to the right of }{\bf b}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\bf i}_{\bf b}(\nu_{2})\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}&\text{if }h\text{ passes from the left to the right of }{\bf b};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\bf i}_{\bf b}(\nu_{2})\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}&\text{if }\bar{h}\text{ passes from the left to the right of }{\bf b}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}(h).

On the other hand, if the barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
that
h
intersects is an isolated edge
e
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, let
{\bf e}
be
e
equipped with the orientation so that
h
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
. Then let
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, i.e.
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the backward and forward endpoints of
{\bf e}
respectively. With these choices (see Proposition
5.8
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(h)=\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}(\nu_{1})\cdot A^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}(\nu_{2})\cdot A^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}(h).

Suppose now that
h
satisfies (ii). Choose an orientation
{\bf e}
on
e
so that
h(0)
lies to the left of
{\bf e}
, and let
{\bf r}:=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
. Then by definition (see Proposition
5.9
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}(\nu_{1})\cdot A^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\bf k}_{\bf e}(\nu_{2})\cdot A^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}.

Then the fact that
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(h)=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}(h)
is an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.11
(2) and the previous paragraph. Finally, if
h
satisfies (iii), then the fact that
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(h)=\widetilde{\nu}_{2}(h)
is an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.11
(1) and the previous paragraph. This finishes the proof that
\Xi
is injective.

Next, we prove that the image of
\Xi
is
\mathscr{W}
. Since
\dim\mathscr{T}=\dim\Hit_{V}(S)=\dim\mathscr{W}
, it suffices to show that the image of
\Xi
lies in
\mathscr{W}
, i.e. if
\nu\in\mathscr{T}
, then

\Xi(\nu)=\left(\left(\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}(\nu)\right)_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A},\widehat{\bf e}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}},\left(\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}(\nu)\right)_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B},\widehat{\bf b}\in\mathcal{M}}\right)=\left(\left(\alpha^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}\right)_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A},\widehat{\bf e}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}},\left(\alpha^{\bf i}_{\widehat{\bf b}}\right)_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B},\widehat{\bf b}\in\mathcal{M}}\right)

satisfy the symmetry for triples, the symmetry of pairs, and the closed leaf equalities (conditions (I), (II), and (III) above). The fact that
\Xi(\nu)
satisfies the symmetry of triples holds is immediate from Proposition
5.5
(1), and the symmetry of pairs holds by Proposition
5.8
(1) and Proposition
5.9
(1).

To see that the closed leaf equalities hold, fixed an oriented non-isolated edge
{\bf e}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
and a bridge
J
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across
{\bf e}
. Let
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, and let
H
,
x
,
\gamma
,
{\bf b}_{j}
,
{\bf e}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}
,
l_{j}
,
m_{j}
,
m
,
H^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
,
{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}
,
{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}^{\prime}
,
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}^{\prime}

l_{j}^{\prime}
,
m_{j}^{\prime}
, and
m^{\prime}
be as defined in Notation
5.10
. Then let
{\bf r}_{j}
and
{\bf r}^{\prime}_{j}
be the pairs associated to
{\bf e}_{j}
and
{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}
respectively, and let
{\bf x}_{j}
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}_{j}
be the triples associated to
{\bf b}_{j}
and
{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}
respectively. Observe that
l_{j}
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}_{j}
if and only if
x=r_{2}
, and
l_{j}^{\prime}
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}
if and only if
x^{\prime}=r_{1}
. Similarly,
m_{j}
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}_{j}
if and only if
x=r_{2}
, and
m_{j}^{\prime}
passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}
if and only if
x^{\prime}=r_{1}
. Thus, by definition (see Proposition
5.5
, (
5.4
), Proposition
5.8
, and (
5.6
)),

\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{2}.\end{array}\right.

For any
k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, let
\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}:i_{1}=k\}
. The next lemma allows us to write down the diagonal part of
\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})
,
\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})
,
\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})
, and
\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})
with respect to
{\bf r}
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
. If
B^{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
is one of the following:

•

\frac{1}{2}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j})}
for some
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}
and
j=1,\dots,H
, or

•

-D^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j})}
for some
j=1,\dots,H
, where
\bar{\bf k}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
for all
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
,

then
B^{\bf k}\cdot\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(x)\subset\xi_{\rho}^{(i)}(x)
, so we may decompose
B^{\mathbf{k}}=B^{\mathbf{k}}_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+B^{\mathbf{k}}_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}
. Furthermore,

B^{\mathbf{k}}_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{1},\\
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\bar{\mathbf{r}})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

where
\bar{\bf r}:=(r_{2},r_{1})
.

Proof.

The first statement of the lemma follows from Proposition
5.4
(1) and Proposition
5.7
(1). To second statement follows from the observation that
B^{\mathbf{k}}
has an eigenbasis whose corresponding eigenvalues are either
\frac{k_{2}}{2n}
or
-\frac{k_{1}}{2n}
, and it scales
\xi_{\rho}^{(k_{1})}(x)
by
\frac{k_{2}}{2n}
.
∎

Since
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}=-D^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\bar{\mathbf{r}})}
for all
{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}
, by Lemma
5.14
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle 2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\bar{\mathbf{r}})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle 2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\bar{\mathbf{r}})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

Similarly,

\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle 2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle 2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

and

\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle 2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle 2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{2}.\end{array}\right.

By definition,

\widetilde{\nu}(m)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\widetilde{\nu}(m^{\prime})&\text{if }x\neq x^{\prime};\\
\widetilde{\nu}(m^{\prime})&\text{if }x=x^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

It then follows from Proposition
5.11
that

\sum_{j=1}^{H}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\sum_{j=1}^{H}\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}-\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}&\text{if }x\neq x^{\prime};\\
\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\widetilde{\nu}(l_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\widetilde{\nu}(m_{j}^{\prime})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}&\text{if }x=x^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

Proposition
5.7
(2) implies that we may compare coefficients to deduce that

\sum_{j=1}^{H}\left(\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\left(\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{2}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\right)&\text{if }x\neq x^{\prime};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{H^{\prime}}\left(\alpha^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{k_{1}}}\alpha^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\right)&\text{if }x=x^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

for all
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
. This proves that
\Xi(\nu)
satisfies the closed leaf equalities.
∎

5.4.
Description of
\Omega
and the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields

Next, we describe the map
\Omega
. First, if
{\bf b}
is a non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the triple associated to
{\bf b}
. Then for any non-edge barrier
{\bf b}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
and any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
, define the function

\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}:\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}

by
\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}[\rho]:=\log T^{\mathbf{i}}(\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}))
and
{\bf x}
is the triple associated to
{\bf b}
. This is well-defined by the projective invariance of the triple ratio and Theorem
2.10
(1). The projective invariance of the triple ratio also implies that
\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}[\rho]=\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\gamma\cdot\mathbf{b}}[\rho]
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, so for every non-edge barrier
\widehat{\bf b}
in
\mathcal{D}
, we may define

\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}[\rho]:=\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{b}}[\rho]

for some (any) lift
{\bf b}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
of
\widehat{\bf b}
.

Similarly, for any oriented edge
\mathbf{e}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
, let
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
. If
{\bf e}
is isolated, let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be the two ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that lie to the left and right of
{\bf e}
respectively, and let
w
and
w^{\prime}
be respectively the vertices of
T
and
T^{\prime}
that are neither
r_{1}
nor
r_{2}
. Then for any
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
, define the function

\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}:\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}

by
\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}[\rho]:=\log\left(-C^{\mathbf{k}}\big(\xi_{\rho}(r_{1}),\xi_{\rho}(w),\xi_{\rho}(r_{2}),\xi_{\rho}(w^{\prime})\big)\right)
. This is well-defined by Theorem
2.10
(2) and the projective invariance of the cross ratio. On the other hand, if
{\bf e}
is non-isolated, let
J
be a bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across
\mathbf{e}
, and let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be the ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contain the endpoints of
J
, so that
T
and
T^{\prime}
lie to the left and right of
\mathbf{e}
respectively. Let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
be the vertices of
T
and
T^{\prime}
respectively, so that
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
are endpoints of
{\bf e}
, while
x_{3}
and
x_{3}^{\prime}
are not vertices of the fragmented ideal triangles that contain the endpoints of
J
. Let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be the endpoint of
{\bf e}
that is not
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
respectively. Then for any
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
, define the function

\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}:\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}

by
\alpha^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}[\rho]:=\log\left(-C^{\mathbf{k}}\big(\xi_{\rho}(r_{1}),u\cdot\xi_{\rho}(x_{3}),\xi_{\rho}(r_{2}),u^{\prime}\cdot\xi_{\rho}(x_{3}^{\prime})\big)\right)
, where
u\in\PGL(V)
(resp.
u^{\prime}\in\PGL(V)
) is the unipotent projective transformations that fixes
\xi(x_{1})
(resp.
\xi(x_{1}^{\prime})
) and sends
\xi(x_{2})
to
\xi(q)
(resp.
\xi(x_{2}^{\prime})
to
\xi(q^{\prime})
). This is well-defined by
[
SWZ20
, Proposition 4.11]
. The projective invariance of the cross ratio then implies that regardless of whether
{\bf e}
is isolated or non-isolated, we have
\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}[\rho]=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}_{\gamma\cdot\mathbf{e}}[\rho]
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, so for every oriented edge
\widehat{\bf e}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}
, we may define

\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}[\rho]=\sigma^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{e}}[\rho].

With this, we can describe the parameterization map
\Omega
in Theorem
5.1
: It is given by

\Omega=\Omega_{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J}}:[\rho]\mapsto\left(\left(\sigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}^{\mathbf{k}}[\rho]\right)_{(\mathbf{k},\widehat{\mathbf{e}})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{T}^{o}},\left(\tau^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}[\rho]\right)_{(\mathbf{i},\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{M}}\right).

Remark 5.15
.

(1)

The convex cone
\mathscr{C}
is an open cone in
\mathscr{W}
cut out by a family of explicit inequalites called the
closed leaf inequalities
(see
[
BD14
, Section 4]
). However, the explicit description of
\mathscr{C}
is not needed in this work, so we do not give it here.

(2)

Bonahon-Dreyer
[
BD14
]
previously constructed a similar parameterization of
\Hit_{V}(S)
. The only difference between this parameterization and the one given by Bonahon-Dreyer parameterization is that when
\widehat{\bf e}
is a non-isolated edge in
\mathcal{T}^{o}
, the invariant
\sigma^{\bf k}_{\widehat{\bf e}}
give here is different from the ones used in Bonahon-Dreyer. While the invariants used in Bonahon-Dreyer are simpler, Theorem
5.1
implies that the ones used here are better for describing the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization, and as we will see later, are more suitable for studying the symplectic structure on
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Using the parameterization
\Omega
, we can define the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields.

Definition 5.16
.

For every
\mu\in\mathscr{W}
, the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field on
\Hit_{V}(S)
associated to
\mu
is a vector field
\mathcal{X}_{\mu}
on
\Hit_{V}(S)
such that
{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}(\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho]))=\mu
for all
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
.

Fix

\mu=\left(\left(\mu_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\right)_{(\mathbf{k},\widehat{\mathbf{e}})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{T}^{o}},\left(\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}\right)_{(\mathbf{i},\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{M}}\right)\in\mathscr{W},

and let
\mathcal{X}_{\mu}
be the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field on
\Hit_{V}(S)
associated to
\mu
. Then for any
\rho\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, let
\nu_{\mu}^{\rho}
be the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle associated to
\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho])
. We will now state two properties of the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}:C_{1}(\widetilde{S},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}(V)
of
\nu_{\mu}^{\rho}
. The first is an immediate consequence of
[
SWZ20
, Proposition 7.12]
and the second follows easily from
[
SWZ20
, Proposition 7.13]
.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the vertices of an ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, let
\mu\in\mathscr{W}
, and let
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
be the path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
such that
[\rho_{t}]=\Omega^{-1}(\Omega([\rho])+t\mu)
(since
\mathscr{C}\subset\mathscr{W}
is open, this is well-defined for sufficiently small
t
).

(1)

Let
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
be the vertices of an ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
such that
x_{1}=y_{2}
,
x_{2}=y_{1}
, and
y_{3}\neq x_{3}
. Let
{\bf e}
be the oriented edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
whose backward and forward endpoints are
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, let
\mathbf{r}
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
(i.e.
{\bf r}:=(x_{1},x_{2})
), let
\widehat{\bf e}:=\pi_{S}({\bf e})
, and let
h
be a
1
-simplex that passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
(see Figure
10
). Then

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\rho_{t})=-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}.

(2)

Let
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
be the vertices of an ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
such that
x_{2}=y_{1}
,
x_{3}=y_{2}
and
x_{1}=y_{3}
. Let
{\bf b}
be the non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
such that
{\bf y}
is associated to
{\bf b}
, let
\widehat{\bf b}:=\pi_{S}({\bf b})
, and let
h
be a
1
-simplex that passes from the left to the right of
{\bf b}
(see Figure
9
). Then

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(\rho_{t})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{y})}.

To describe the second, recall that if
{\bf e}
is an oriented, non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
,
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2})
is the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, and
J\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
is a bridge across
{\bf e}
, then
H
,
x
,
\gamma
,
{\bf b}_{j}
,
{\bf e}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}
,
l_{j}
,
m_{j}
,
m
,
H^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
,
{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}
,
{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}
,
l_{j}^{\prime}
,
m_{j}^{\prime}
, and
m^{\prime}
were defined in Notation
5.10
. Let
{\bf r}_{j}
and
{\bf r}^{\prime}_{j}
be the pairs associated to
{\bf e}_{j}
and
{\bf e}^{\prime}_{j}
respectively, and let
{\bf x}_{j}
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}_{j}
be the triples associated to
{\bf b}_{j}
and
{\bf b}^{\prime}_{j}
respectively. Then set

A_{j}(\mu):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

D_{j}(\mu):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j})}&\text{if }x=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

A_{j}^{\prime}(\mu):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

D_{j}^{\prime}(\mu):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{1};\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}2\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r}_{j}^{\prime})}&\text{if }x^{\prime}=r_{2},\end{array}\right.

and set

(5.10)

\displaystyle N_{\infty}(\mu,{\bf r},J):=\sum_{j=1}^{H}A_{j}(\mu)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\sum_{j=1}^{H}D_{j}(\mu)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}

and

\displaystyle N_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mu,{\bf r},J):=\sum_{j=1}^{H}A_{j}^{\prime}(\mu)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\sum_{j=1}^{H}D_{j}^{\prime}(\mu)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
{\bf e}
be an oriented, non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
, let
\widehat{\bf e}:=\pi_{S}({\bf e})
, let
{\bf r}
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, let
J\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
be a bridge across
{\bf e}
, and let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be the ideal triangles of
\mathcal{T}
that contain the endpoints of
J
and lie to the left and right of
{\bf e}
respectively. Let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
be the vertices of
T
and
T^{\prime}
respectively, so that
x_{1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
are endpoints of
{\bf e}
, while
x_{3}
and
x_{3}^{\prime}
are not vertices of the fragmented ideal triangles that contain the endpoints of
J
. Let
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
be the
1
-simplex such that
h(0)
and
h(1)
are the endpoints of
J
that lie in
T
and
T^{\prime}
respectively. If
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
is the path in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,{\bf x}}
such that
[\rho_{t}]=\Omega^{-1}(\Omega([\rho])+t\mu)
, then

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}g_{{\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}}(\rho_{t})=N_{\infty}(\mu,\mathbf{r},J)-N^{\prime}_{\infty}(\mu,\mathbf{r},J)-2\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}.

We may now prove Theorem
5.2
.

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

By Proposition
5.13
, we know that
\Xi
is an embedding whose image is
\mathscr{W}
. It follows from Propositions
5.17
and
5.18
that the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}
of the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\nu_{\mu}^{\rho}:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho]))
satisfies the following:

•

If
{\bf b}
is a non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
,
\widehat{\bf b}:=\pi_{S}({\bf b})
,
{\bf x}
is the triple associated to
{\bf b}
, and
h
is a
1
-simplex that passes from the left to the right
{\bf b}
, then

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi(\mathbf{x})}.

•

If
{\bf e}
is an oriented, isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
,
\widehat{\bf e}:=\pi_{S}({\bf e})
,
{\bf r}
is the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, and
h
is a
1
-simplex that passes from the left to the right of
{\bf e}
, then

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=-2\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}.

•

If
{\bf e}
is an oriented, non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
,
\widehat{\bf e}:=\pi_{S}({\bf e})
,
{\bf r}
is the pair associated to
{\bf e}
,
J
is a bridge across
{\bf e}
, and
h
is a
1
-simplex such that
h(0)
is the endpoint of
J
that lies to the left of
{\bf e}
and
h(1)=e\cap J
, then

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}=-2\sum_{{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}}\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}.

Then from the definition of
\Xi
, it follows that
\Xi\circ\Phi(\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho]))=\mu
.
∎

6.
The
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization of
\Hit_{V}(S)
is symplectic

In this section, we will apply Theorem
5.2
to show that the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization of
T\Hit_{V}(S)
is symplectic.

Theorem 6.1
.

There is a symplectic bilinear form
\omega_{\mathscr{W}}
on the vector space
\mathscr{W}
for which the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-trivialization
{\rm d}\Omega:T\Hit_{V}(S)\to T\mathscr{C}\cong\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{W}
is symplectic.

Theorem
6.1
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 6.2
.

Every
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field on
\Hit_{V}(S)
is a Hamiltonian vector field.

Proof.

By definition, every
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field is identified via
{\rm d}\Omega
with a constant vector field on
T\mathscr{C}
. Since constant vector fields on an open set in a symplectic vector space are Hamiltonian vector fields, the corollary follows from Theorem
6.1
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
6.1
will be given in Section
6.1
and Section
6.2
.

6.1.
A triangulation of
S

In order to prove Theorem
6.1
, we need to compute the Goldman symplectic form using (
2.9
), and to do so, we need to choose a finite triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of the surface that is well adapted to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{J}
. We now describe this choice.

Choose a point
q_{e}
on each isolated edge
e\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, such that
\gamma\cdot q_{e}=q_{\gamma\cdot e}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Then for each ideal triangle
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
be the edges of
T
, and let
\delta(T)
be the geodesic triangle in
T
whose vertices are
q_{e_{1}},q_{e_{2}},q_{e_{3}}
, see Figure
12
. (Each triangle
\delta(T)
is an open set in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
.) Observe that
\gamma\cdot\delta(T)=\delta(\gamma\cdot T)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Thus, we may denote
\delta(\widehat{T}):=\pi_{S}(\delta(T))
, where
