\pi_{S}:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
is the covering map and
\widehat{T}:=\pi_{S}(T)
.

Figure 12.
T
and
\delta(T)
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}
) denote the set of non-isolated edges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}
), and let
\widetilde{\Theta}
(resp.
\Theta
) denote the set of ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}
).

Definition 6.3
.

(1)

A
boundary cylinder
is a connected component of

\Sigma\bigg\backslash\left(\bigcup_{\widehat{T}\in\Theta}\overline{\delta(\widehat{T})}\cup\bigcup_{\widehat{e}\in\mathcal{P}}\widehat{e}\right).

(2)

A
lifted boundary cylinder
is a connected component of

\widetilde{\Sigma}\bigg\backslash\left(\bigcup_{T\in\widetilde{\Theta}}\overline{\delta(T)}\cup\bigcup_{e\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}e\right).

Observe that the boundary cylinders are topological cylinders. Also, every lifted boundary cylinder
C
has two boundary components in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, one of which is a non-isolated edge
e_{C}
, and the other is a piecewise geodesic, where each of the geodesic pieces are edges of
\delta(T)
for some ideal triangle
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. Furthermore, for every lifted boundary cylinder
C
,
\pi_{S}|_{C}:C\to\widehat{C}
is a covering map from
C
to some boundary cylinder
\widehat{C}:=\pi_{S}(C)
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
C
be a lifted boundary cylinder.

(1)

For every bridge
J\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across
e_{C}
, let
q_{J}:=J\cap e_{C}
. Also, let
T=T_{C,J}
be the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that contains an endpoint of
J
, such that the (unique) edge
e=e_{C,J}
of
T
that intersects
J
lies in the boundary of
C
. The
bridge parallel edge

s_{C,J}
in
C
corresponding to
J
is the geodesic segment whose endpoints are
q_{J}
and
q_{e}
, see Figure
13
.

(2)

A
fundamental block
in a lifted boundary cylinder
C
is a connected component of

C-\left(\bigcup_{\{J\in\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}:J\cap e_{C}\neq\emptyset\}}s_{C,J}\right).

Note that the boundary
\partial D
in
\widetilde{S}
of any fundamental block
D
is a topological circle that contains two bridge parallel edges, and the complement in
\partial D
of these two bridge parallel edges are two connected components. One of these components lies in
e_{C}
, while the other is a piecewise geodesic segment
f_{D}
with finitely many pieces, each of which is the edge of
\delta(T)
for some ideal triangle
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.

Figure 13.
The isolated edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
are drawn in blue, the non-isolated edge
e\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is drawn in thick red, and the bridges across
e
is drawn in dotted green. The thin orange lines are the edges of the triangulation
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
that lie in two fundamental blocks that share a common boundary segment in
e
.

For each fundamental block
D
of a lifted boundary cylinder
C
, choose a point
b_{D}
in the interior of the subsegment of
\partial D
that lies in
e_{C}
, so that
b_{\gamma\cdot D}=\gamma\cdot b_{D}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and
b_{D}=b_{D^{\prime}}
if
D
and
D^{\prime}
are fundamental blocks whose closures intersect along a subsegment of
e_{C}
, see Figure
13
. Then let
\mathbb{V}_{D}
denote the set of endpoints of the geodesic segments in
f_{D}
, and let
\mathbb{F}_{D}
be the set of geodesic segments in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
that have
b_{D}
and a point in
\mathbb{V}_{D}
as endpoints. Observe that the geodesic segments in
\mathbb{F}_{D}
define a triangulation of
D
. Also, these geodesic segments, together with the bridge parallel edges in
C
, define a triangulation of
C
that is invariant under the deck group, and thus defines a triangulation of the boundary cylinder
\widehat{C}
. Let
\mathbb{T}(D)
,
\mathbb{T}(C)
, and
\mathbb{T}(\widehat{C})
denote the set of triangles in this triangulation of
D
,
C
, and
\widehat{C}
respectively.

Let
\mathcal{C}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
) denote the set of boundary cylinders in
\Sigma
(resp. lifted boundary cylinders in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
). Recall that
\Theta
(resp.
\widetilde{\Theta}
) denote the set of ideal triangles of
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
). It is clear from our definitions that

\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}:=\{\delta(T):T\in\widetilde{\Theta}\}\cup\bigcup_{C\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}\mathbb{T}(C)

is a
\Gamma
-invariant triangulation of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, which descends to the triangulation

\mathbb{T}:=\{\delta(\widehat{T}):\widehat{T}\in\Theta\}\cup\bigcup_{\widehat{C}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{T}(\widehat{C})

of
\Sigma
. We refer to any triangulation
\mathbb{T}
constructed above as a triangulation of
\Sigma
that is
well adapted
to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
.

6.2.
The cup product of parallel vector fields

We will use the following terminology in the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a triangulation of
\Sigma
that is well adapted to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
, and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
denote its lift to
\widetilde{\Sigma}
.

(1)

An
internal triangle
of
\mathbb{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
) is a triangle of the form
\delta(\widehat{T})
(resp.
\delta(T)
) for some ideal triangle
\widehat{T}
of
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
T
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
).

(2)

An
external triangle
of
\mathbb{T}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
) is a triangle in
\mathbb{T}(\widehat{C})
(resp.
\mathbb{T}(C)
) for some boundary cylinder
\widehat{C}
in
\Sigma
(resp. lifted boundary cylinder
C
in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
).

(3)

An edge of a triangle in
\mathbb{T}
(or
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
) is

•

an
internal edge
if both of its vertices are vertices of internal triangles.

•

an
external edge
if both of its vertices lie in a non-isolated edge.

•

a
crossing edge
if one of its vertices is a vertex of an internal triangle, and the other vertex lies in a non-isolated edge.

Note that every edge of a triangle in
\mathbb{T}
is either an internal edge, an external edge, or a crossing edge. Also, all bridge parallel edges are crossing edges, and the boundary of every fundamental block contains two bridge parallel edges.

Recall that
\mathcal{A}
denotes the set of pairs of positive integers that sum to
n
and
\mathcal{B}
denotes the set of triples of positive integers that sum to
n
. Also, recall that for any generic pair of flags
\mathbf{E}:=(E_{1},E_{2})
in
\mathcal{F}(V)
and
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
, we defined the shearing endomorphism
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{E}}
, see Definition
5.6
. Similarly, for any generic triple of flags
\mathbf{F}:=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
in
\mathcal{F}(V)
and any
{\bf i}\in\mathcal{B}
, we defined the eruption endomorphisms
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
, see Definition
5.3
. To prove Theorem
6.1
, it is also convenient to use the following notation.

Notation 6.6
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
denote the set of triples of integers
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
that sum to
n
, and satisfy
0\leq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\leq n-1
.

(1)

We can extend the eruption endomorphisms from
\mathcal{B}
to all of
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
by setting

•

A^{(i_{1},i_{2},0)}_{\mathbf{F}}
to be the endomorphism whose eigenspaces are
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}
and
F_{2}^{(i_{2})}
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\frac{i_{2}}{n}
and
-\frac{i_{1}}{n}
respectively, i.e.
A^{(i_{1},i_{2},0)}_{\mathbf{F}}=2D^{(i_{1},i_{2})}_{(F_{1},F_{2})}
,

•

A^{(0,i_{2},i_{3})}_{\mathbf{F}}
to be the zero endomorphism,

•

A^{(i_{1},0,i_{3})}_{\mathbf{F}}
to be the endomorphism whose eigenspaces are
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}
and
F_{3}^{(i_{3})}
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\frac{i_{3}}{n}
and
-\frac{i_{1}}{n}
respectively, i.e.
A^{(i_{1},0,i_{3})}_{\mathbf{F}}=2D^{(i_{1},i_{3})}_{(F_{1},F_{3})}
.

(2)

Given a non-edge barrier
\widehat{\bf b}
of
\mathcal{D}
, let
{\bf b}
be a non-edge barrier in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\pi_{S}({\bf b})=\widehat{\bf b}
, and let
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
be the triple associated to
{\bf b}
. For all
l=1,2,3
, let
{\bf e}_{l}
be the oriented edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{o}
whose backward and forward endpoints are
x_{l+1}
and
x_{l-1}
, and let
\widehat{\bf e}_{l}:=\pi_{S}({\bf e}_{l})
. For any
\mathbf{k}:=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
, set
\mathbf{k}_{1}:=(0,k_{1},k_{2})
,
\mathbf{k}_{2}:=(k_{2},0,k_{1})
, and
\mathbf{k}_{3}:=(k_{1},k_{2},0)
. Then for any vector

\mu=\left(\left(\mu_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\right)_{(\mathbf{k},\widehat{\mathbf{e}})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{T}^{o}},\left(\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}\right)_{(\mathbf{i},\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{M}}\right)\in\mathscr{W},

set

\mu_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}^{\mathbf{k}_{l}}:=\mu_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{l}}^{\mathbf{k}}

for all
l=1,2,3
.

In this notation, if
h:[0,1]\to\widetilde{S}
is the
1
-simplex such that
h(0)
lies in
{\bf e}_{2}
and
h(1)
lies in
{\bf e}_{3}
, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(-\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(x_{3},x_{1})}-\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\bar{\mathbf{e}}}_{3}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(x_{2},x_{1})}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}

(6.1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\left(\mu^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(x_{1},x_{3})}+\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}}D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(x_{1},x_{2})}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})},

where
\bar{\bf k}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
and
\widehat{\bar{\mathbf{e}}}_{3}
is the
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
with its orientation reversed. Here, the first equality holds by definition (see (
4.7
)) and Proposition
5.17
, and the second equality holds because
\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\bar{\mathbf{e}}}_{3}}=\mu^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}}
(see Proposition
5.8
(1))
D^{\mathbf{k}}_{{\bf E}}=-D^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}_{\bar{\bf E}}
for all
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
and all transverse pairs of flags
{\bf E}=(E_{1},E_{2})
, and . and
\bar{\bf E}:=(E_{2},E_{1})

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Fix a triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
\Sigma
that is well adapted to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
, and choose an enumeration on the set of vertices
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{6g-6+2|\mathcal{P}|}\}
of
\mathbb{T}
, where
g
is the genus of
S
. This induces an orientation on every edge of
\mathbb{T}
in the following way: if
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
are the endpoints of an edge of
\mathbb{T}
, then
v_{i}
is the backward endpoint and
v_{j}
is the forward endpoint if and only if
i<j
.

Now, for any triangle
\widehat{\delta}\in\mathbb{T}
, choose a triangle
\delta\in\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
such that
\pi_{S}(\delta)=\widehat{\delta}
. Then let
\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
denote the set of all such triangles in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, one for each triangle in
\mathbb{T}
. For any triangle
\delta\in\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
, let
v_{a},v_{b},v_{c}
be the vertices of
\pi_{S}(\delta)
such that
a<b<c
, and let
e_{\delta,1}
and
e_{\delta,2}
denote the oriented edges of
\delta
such that the backward and forward endpoints of
\pi_{S}(e_{\delta,1})
are
v_{a}
and
v_{b}
respectively, and the backward and forward endpoints of
\pi_{S}(e_{\delta,2})
are
v_{b}
and
v_{c}
respectively.

Let
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
be a pair of vectors in
\mathscr{W}
, and let
\mathcal{X}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mu^{\prime}}
be respectively the corresponding
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
. Let
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
is a Hitchin representation. Then let
\nu_{\mu}^{\rho}
and
\nu_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}
be the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles of the tangent vectors
\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho])
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mu^{\prime}}([\rho])
in
T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
respectively, and let
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}
be the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of
\nu_{\mu}^{\rho}
and
\nu_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}
respectively. By (
2.9
),

(6.2)

\omega_{[\rho]}\big(\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}_{\mu^{\prime}}([\rho])\big)=\sum_{\delta\in\widehat{\mathbb{T}}}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})\big).

Thus, it suffices to show that for all
\delta\in\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
the function
\widetilde{\Hit}_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\rho\mapsto\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})\big)

is constant. Indeed, if this were true, then by (
6.2
), the function
\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

[\rho]\mapsto\omega_{[\rho]}\big(\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}_{\mu^{\prime}}([\rho])\big)

is constant, so we may define the required symplectic bilinear form
\omega_{\mathscr{W}}
on
\mathscr{W}
by

\omega_{\mathscr{W}}(\mu,\mu^{\prime}):=\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}_{\mu}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}_{\mu^{\prime}}([\rho])\right)

for some (any)
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
.

The following lemma is the key computation needed to prove that for any
\delta\in\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
,
\rho\mapsto\tr\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})\right)
is constant.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\mathbf{F}:=(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
be a transverse triple of flags in
\mathcal{F}(V)
and
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
and
\mathbf{j}:=(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})
in
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

\displaystyle\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\right)=[\min\{i_{1}-j_{1},j_{2}-i_{2}\}]_{+}-\frac{i_{1}j_{2}}{n}
,

(2)

\displaystyle\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}\right)=\min\{i_{1},j_{1}\}-\frac{i_{1}j_{1}}{n}
.

where
\mathbf{j}_{+}:=(j_{2},j_{3},j_{1})
,
\mathbf{F}_{+}:=(F_{2},F_{3},F_{1})
, and
[c]_{+}:=\max\{0,c\}
for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular,
\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\right)
and
\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}\right)
do not depend on
\mathbf{F}
.

Proof.

For
m=1,2,3
, let
\{f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,n}\}
be a basis of
V
such that

F_{m}^{(j)}=\Span\{f_{m,1},\dots,f_{m,j}\}

for all
j=1,\dots,n-1
.

(1) For any
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
, let
E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}:=A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}+\frac{i_{1}}{n}\cdot\id\in\End(V)
, and note that
E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
has
F_{1}^{(i_{1})}
and
F_{2}^{(i_{2})}+F_{3}^{(i_{3})}
as eigenspaces corresponding to eigenvalues
1
and
0
respectively. This implies that

(6.3)

\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\right)=\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}.\right)-\frac{i_{1}j_{2}}{n}

for all
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
and
\mathbf{j}:=(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})
in
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
.

The proof of (1) will be divided into the following four cases:

(i)

i_{1}\leq j_{1}
,

(ii)

j_{2}\leq i_{2}
,

(iii)

i_{1}>j_{1}
,
j_{2}>i_{2}
and
i_{3}\geq j_{3}
,

(iv)

i_{1}>j_{1}
,
j_{2}>i_{2}
and
j_{3}\geq i_{3}
.

We show that in all four cases,
\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}.\right)=[\min\{i_{1}-j_{1},j_{2}-i_{2}\}]_{+}
. This, together with (
6.3
), proves (1).

If (i) holds, then every vector in the basis

\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}\}

lies in the kernel of
E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
, so
E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}=0
. Similarly, if (ii) holds, then every vector in the basis

\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,j_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,j_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,j_{3}}\}

lies in the kernel of
E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}
, so
E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}=0
. Thus, if (i) or (ii) holds, then

\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\right)=\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\right)=0=[\min\{i_{1}-j_{1},j_{2}-i_{2}\}]_{+}.

Suppose that (iii) holds. In the basis
\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}\}
, the endomorphism
E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
can be written as the diagonal matrix

E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\id_{i_{1}}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right).

For any
k
such that
j_{1}+1\leq k\leq i_{1}
, we may write

f_{1,k}=\sum_{s=1}^{j_{1}}a_{1,s}f_{1,s}+\sum_{s=1}^{j_{2}}a_{2,s}f_{2,s}+\sum_{s=1}^{j_{3}}a_{3,s}f_{3,s}

for some
a_{m,s}\in\mathbb{R}
. One can compute that

E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\cdot f_{1,k}=f_{1,k}-\left(\sum_{s=1}^{j_{1}}a_{1,s}f_{1,s}+\sum_{s=1}^{j_{3}}a_{3,s}f_{3,s}\right).

Similarly, for any
l
such that
j_{3}+1\leq l\leq i_{3}
, we also have

E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\cdot f_{3,l}=f_{3,l}-\left(\sum_{s=1}^{j_{1}}b_{1,s}f_{1,s}+\sum_{s=1}^{j_{3}}b_{3,s}f_{3,s}\right)

for some
b_{m,s}\in\mathbb{R}
. This implies that in the basis

\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}\},

the endomorphism
E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}
can be written as the matrix

E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0\cdot\id_{j_{1}}&*&0&0&*\\
0&\id_{i_{1}-j_{1}}&0&0&0\\
0&0&\id_{i_{2}}&0&0\\
0&*&0&0\cdot\id_{j_{3}}&*\\
0&0&0&0&\id_{i_{3}-j_{3}}\end{array}\right).

It is then a straightforward computation to show that

\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\right)=i_{1}-j_{1}.

Furthermore,
i_{3}\geq j_{3}
implies that
i_{1}-j_{1}\leq j_{2}-i_{2}
, which implies that

\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}\right)=[\min\{i_{1}-j_{1},j_{2}-i_{2}\}]_{+}.

By switching the roles of
E^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
and
E^{\mathbf{j}_{+}}_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}
, and using the basis

\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,j_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,j_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,j_{3}}\}

in place of

\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,i_{1}},f_{2,1},\dots,f_{2,i_{2}},f_{3,1},\dots,f_{3,i_{3}}\}

in the argument in the previous paragraph, we see that if (iv) holds, then

\tr\left(E^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{\mathbf{F}_{1}}\cdot E^{\mathbf{j}_{2}}_{\mathbf{F}_{2}}\right)=j_{2}-i_{2}=[\min\{i_{1}-j_{1},j_{2}-i_{2}\}]_{+}.

(2) By Proposition
5.4
(1), both
A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}
and
A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}
fix the flag
F
. Thus, in the basis
\{f_{1,1},\dots,f_{1,n}\}
, they can be represented by the matrices

A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{i_{2}+i_{3}}{n}\id_{i_{1}}&*\\
0&U_{i_{2}+i_{3}}\end{array}\right)\text{ and }A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{j_{2}+j_{3}}{n}\id_{j_{1}}&*\\
0&U_{j_{2}+j_{3}}\end{array}\right)

where
U_{i_{2}+i_{3}}
(resp.
U_{j_{2}+j_{3}}
) is a
(i_{2}+i_{3})\times(i_{2}+i_{3})
(resp.
(j_{2}+j_{3})\times(j_{2}+j_{3})
) upper triangular matrix whose diagonal entries are all
-\frac{i_{1}}{n}
(resp.
-\frac{j_{1}}{n}
). Then

\displaystyle\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\mathbf{F}}\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{i_{2}+i_{3}}{n}\id_{i_{1}}&0\\
0&-\frac{i_{1}}{n}\id_{i_{2}+i_{3}}\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{j_{2}+j_{3}}{n}\id_{j_{1}}&0\\
0&-\frac{j_{1}}{n}\id_{j_{2}+j_{3}}\end{array}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\id_{i_{1}}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right]-\frac{i_{1}}{n}\id\right)\cdot\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\id_{j_{1}}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right]-\frac{j_{1}}{n}\id\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\min\{i_{1},j_{1}\}-\frac{i_{1}j_{1}}{n}.\qed

Recall that any
\delta\in\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
is either an internal triangle or an external triangle. We deal with these two cases separately in the following pair of lemmas.

Lemma 6.8
.

If
\delta\in\widehat{T}
is an external triangle, then the function

\rho\mapsto\tr\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})\right)

is constant.

Proof.

Let
D
denote the fundamental block containing
\delta
, let
{\bf e}
be the non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
that intersects
\partial D
, oriented so that
D
lies to the left of
{\bf e}
, and let
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2})
be the pair associated to
{\bf e}
, i.e.
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are respectively the backward and forward endpoints of
{\bf e}
. Let
s
be the bridge parallel edge in
\partial D
so that
\gamma\cdot s
also lies in
\partial D
, where
\gamma
is as defined in Notation
5.10
. Then let
H
,
x
,
{\bf b}_{j}
,
{\bf e}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf b}_{j}
,
\widehat{\bf e}_{j}
,
l_{j}
,
m_{j}
, and
m
be as defined in Notation
5.10
. Let
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
be the two external edges in
\partial D
, and let
\mathbb{G}
denote the set of internal edges in the boundary of
D
. Since all of these edges in
\mathbb{T}(D)
are oriented according to the enumeration of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
, we may view them as
1
-simplices in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, in which case they can all be written as a linear combination of the
1
-simplices in
\mathbb{G}\cup\big\{t_{1},t_{2},s\big\}
. Thus, the endomorphism
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,l})
(resp.
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,l})
) is a linear combination of endomorphisms in

\left\{\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h):h\in\mathbb{G}\cup\big\{t_{1},t_{2},s\big\}\right\}\quad\left(\text{resp.}\quad\left\{\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h):h\in\mathbb{G}\cup\big\{t_{1},t_{2},s\big\}\right\}\right),

where the coefficients do not depend on
\rho
. It now suffices to show that the map

\rho\mapsto\tr\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h_{1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h_{2})\right)

is constant for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{G}\cup\big\{t_{1},t_{2},s\big\}
.

Fix a basis
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
f_{i}\in\xi^{(i)}(r_{1})\cap\xi^{(n-i+1)}(r_{2})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Observe that by definition (see (
4.5
)), for all integers
j
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(m_{j})
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{j})
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(m_{j})
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(l_{j})
are all represented in this basis by upper triangular matrices if
x=r_{1}
and lower triangular matrices if
x=r_{2}
. Thus, we have decompositions

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h)=\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}

into the diagonal and nilpotent part with respect to
{\bf r}
(see (
5.5
)). Then:

•

By definition (see (
4.7
)), for all
h\in\mathbb{G}
, there is some integer
j
such that

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)=\epsilon_{h}\left(\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{j-1})+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(m_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{j})\right)

and

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h)=\epsilon_{h}\left(\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(l_{j-1})+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(m_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(l_{j})\right),

where

\epsilon_{h}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{ if $h$ passes from the left to the right of $m_{j}$},\\
-1&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

By definition (see (
4.12
)) and Proposition
5.11
(1), for both
l=1,2
,

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(t_{l})=\frac{\epsilon_{l}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{H}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}

and

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(t_{l})=\frac{\epsilon_{l}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{H}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}},

where

\epsilon_{l}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{ if the orientation on $t_{l}$ agrees with the orientation on $m$},\\
-1&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

By definition (see (
4.7
)) and Proposition
5.11
(2),

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}=-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\right)

and

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}=-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(l_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(l_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}+\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(m_{j})_{{\bf r},{\rm nil}}\right).

Thus, for all
h\in\mathbb{G}\cup\big\{t_{1},t_{2},s\big\}
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)
is also represented in the basis
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
by upper triangular matrices if
x=r_{1}
and lower triangular matrices if
x=r_{2}
. It thus suffices to show that the map

\rho\mapsto\tr\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(h_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\right)

is constant for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{G}\cup\big\{t_{1},t_{2},s\big\}
.

By (
6.6
), if
h\in\mathbb{G}
, then there is some
j\in\{1,\dots,H\}
such that
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)
is a linear combination of
\{A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{j})}:{\bf i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\}
where the coefficients depend only on
\mu
. Then by Lemma
5.14
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(h)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
is a linear combination of
\{D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}:{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}\}
, where the coefficients depend only on
\mu
. At the same time, since
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(t_{1})
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(t_{2})
are linear combinations of the diagonal parts (with respect to
{\bf r}
) of
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(l_{j})
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(m_{j})
over all
j\in\{1,\dots,H\}
, it follows from Proposition
5.17
that both
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(t_{1})
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(t_{2})
are linear combinations of
\{D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}:{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}\}
, where the coefficients depend only on
\mu
. Finally, by Proposition
5.18
and Lemma
5.14
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(s)_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}
is also a linear combination of
\{D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}:{\bf k}\in\mathcal{A}\}
, where the coefficients depend only on
\mu
.

To finish the proof, it now suffices to verify that the map

\rho\mapsto\tr\left(D^{\mathbf{k}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}\cdot D^{\mathbf{l}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{r})}\right)

is constant for all
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
and
{\bf l}=(l_{1},l_{2})
in
\mathcal{A}
. This is immediate from Lemma
6.7
(2).
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

If
\delta\in\widehat{T}
is an internal triangle, then the function

[\rho]\mapsto\tr\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})\right)

is constant.

Proof.

Let
T
be the ideal triangle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
such that contains
\delta
, and let
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
, and
x_{3}
be the vertices of
T
such that
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is cyclically ordered and the non-edge barrier
{\bf b}
in
T
with
x_{1}
as its terminal endpoint intersects
e_{\delta,1}
. Then for
l=1,2,3
, let
e_{l}
be the geodesic whose endpoints are
e_{l+1}
and
e_{l-1}
.

Recall that
{\bf b}_{+}
, and
{\bf b}_{-}
denote the non-edge barriers in
T
whose terminal endpoints are
x_{2}
, and
x_{3}
respectively, and
{\bf x}_{+}
and
{\bf x}_{-}
denote the triples associated to
{\bf b}_{+}
, and
{\bf b}_{-}
respectively. For both
k=1,2
, let
e_{\delta,k}^{+}
and
e_{\delta,k}^{-}
denote the forward and backward endpoints of
e_{\delta,k}
respectively. Then by (
6.6
)

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}&\text{if }e_{\delta,1}^{+}\in e_{3}\\
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}-\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}&\text{if }e_{\delta,1}^{+}\in e_{2}\end{array}\right.

and

\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}&\text{if }e_{\delta,2}^{-}\in e_{3}\\
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{-}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}-\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{-}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{-})}&\text{if }e_{\delta,2}^{-}\in e_{2}\end{array}\right.

It thus follows from Lemma
6.7
(1) that
\tr\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\rho}(e_{\delta,2})\right)
does not depend on
\rho
.
∎

Together, Lemma
6.9
, Lemma
6.8
finishes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
∎

7.
A symplectic basis of vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)

In this section, we consider a particular ideal triangulation and compatible bridge system
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
on
S
that is subordinate to a given pants decomposition on
S
, see Section
7.1
. In the companion paper
[
SWZ20
]
the authors used such a pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
to construct a family of
(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)

special

(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields on
\Hit_{V}(S)
(see
[
SWZ20
, Definition 8.12]
and Remark
7.7
below). They then used these special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel fields to construct an associated global coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
(see
[
SWZ20
, Corollary 8.15]
).

The goal of this section is to explicitly compute the symplectic pairing between the tangent vector fields of any pair of special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel flows. As a consequence, we prove that the global coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
constructed from the special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-vector fields is a global Darboux coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
.

7.1.
Choice of ideal triangulation and compatible bridge system

We begin by recalling what it means for an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
and a compatible bridge system
\mathcal{J}
to be subordinate to a pants decomposition.

Recall that we have fixed a hyperbolic metric on
S
. Choose a pants decomposition on
S
, i.e. a maximal collection of pairwise disjoint, simple, closed geodesics on
S
. These cut the surface into
2g-2
pairs of pants
\mathbb{P}=\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{2g-2}\}
. For each
P\in\mathbb{P}
, choose peripheral group elements
\alpha_{P,1},\alpha_{P,2},\alpha_{P,3}\in\pi_{1}(P)
such that
\alpha_{P,1}\alpha_{P,2}\alpha_{P,3}=\id
, and
P
lies to the right of its boundary components, oriented according to
\alpha_{P,1}
,
\alpha_{P,2}
and
\alpha_{P,3}
(see Figure
14
).

By choosing base points, the inclusion
P\subset S
induces an inclusion
\pi_{1}(P)\subset\Gamma
, so we can view
\alpha_{P,1},\alpha_{P,2},\alpha_{P,3}
as group elements in
\Gamma
. For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, denote the repellor and attractor of
\gamma
in
\partial\Gamma
by
\gamma^{-}
and
\gamma^{+}
respectively. Then let
e_{P,1}
,
e_{P,2}
,
e_{P,3}
,
c_{P,1}
,
c_{P,2}
, and
c_{P,3}
be geodesics in
\widetilde{S}
such that for all
l=1,2,3
, the endpoints of
e_{P,l}
are
\alpha_{P,l-1}^{-}
and
\alpha_{P,l+1}^{-}
, and the endpoints of
c_{P,l}
are
\alpha_{P,l}^{-}
and
\alpha_{P,l}^{+}
. Observe that

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}:=\bigcup_{P\in\mathbb{P}}\Gamma\cdot\big\{e_{P,1},e_{P,2},e_{P,3},c_{P,1},c_{P,2},c_{P,3}\big\}.

is an ideal triangulation, does not depend on the choice of base points, and is
\Gamma
-invariant. Define
\mathcal{T}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}/\Gamma
, and observe that the set of non-isolated edges
\mathcal{P}
in
\mathcal{T}
is canonically identified with the chosen pants decomposition on
S
. Also, note that the set
\widetilde{\Theta}
of ideal triangles of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
can be described as

\widetilde{\Theta}=\bigcup_{P\in\mathbb{P}}\Gamma\cdot\big\{T_{P},T_{P}^{\prime}\big\},

where
T_{P}
is the ideal triangle in
\widetilde{S}
whose vertices are
\alpha_{P,1}^{-}
,
\alpha_{P,2}^{-}
, and
\alpha_{P,3}^{-}
, while
T_{P}^{\prime}
is the ideal triangle in
\widetilde{S}
whose vertices are
\alpha_{P,1}^{-}
,
\alpha_{P,3}^{-}
, and
\alpha_{P,3}\cdot\alpha_{P,2}^{-}
. We also let
\widehat{T}_{P}:=\pi_{S}(T_{P})
and
\widehat{T}_{P}^{\prime}:=\pi_{S}(T_{P}^{\prime})
, where
\pi_{S}:\widetilde{S}\to S
is the covering map.

Figure 14.
The two pairs of pants
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
drawn on the left share a common boundary edge in
\mathcal{P}
. On the right, the non-isolated and isolated edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
are drawn in thick red and blue respectively, while the bridges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
are drawn in dotted green.

Next, fix a bridge system
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
on
\widetilde{S}
compatible with
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, such that every endpoint of every bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
lies in an ideal triangle of the form
\gamma\cdot T_{P}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
and some
P\in\mathbb{P}
. Then let
\mathcal{J}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}/\Gamma
.

Definition 7.1
.

Any pair
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
as described above is said to be
subordinate to the pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
.

7.2.
Special parallel vector fields

Let
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
be an ideal triangulation and compatible bridge system on
S
that is subordinate to a pants decomposition of
S
. Recall that
\mathcal{M}
denotes the set of non-edge barriers associated to
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{T}
denotes the set of non-isolated edges (which in this case is a pants decomposition of
S
), and
\mathcal{P}^{o}\subset\mathcal{T}^{o}
denote the set of oriented, non-isolated edges. Also, recall that
\mathcal{A}
denotes the set of pairs of positive integers that sum to
n
,
\mathcal{B}
denotes the set of triples of positive integers that sum to
n
, and
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
denotes the set of triples of integers
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})
that sum to
n
, such that
0\leq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\leq n-1
.

In the companion paper
[
SWZ20
]
, the special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields were given by explicitly specifying the vector
\mu\in\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{W}_{\mathcal{T}}
that corresponds to each of them via the isomorphism
{\rm d\Omega}
, see Theorem
5.1
. We now recall these vectors
\mu
. Using Notation
6.6
to specify the vector
\mu
, we need only to specify

\left(\mu^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\bf c}}\right)_{(\mathbf{k},\widehat{\bf c})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{P}^{o}}\text{ and }\left(\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}\right)_{(\mathbf{i},\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}}.

Of course, one needs to ensure that if
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
are non-edge barriers whose associated triples
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}:=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime})
satisfy
x_{1}=x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}=x_{3}^{\prime}
, then
\mu^{(k_{1},k_{2},0)}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}=\mu^{(k_{1},0,k_{2})}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}
for all
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
.

We first describe the vectors
\mu
associated to two types of special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields, called the
eruption vector fields
and
hexagon vector fields
. These are vector fields associated to each pair of pants
P\in\mathbb{P}
. To do so, it is convenient to introduce the following notions.

Definition 7.2
.

A
marked pair of pants given by
\mathcal{P}
is a pair
{\bf P}=(P,\widehat{c})
such that
P
is a pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
and
\widehat{c}
is a boundary component of
P
. The
pair
(\widehat{\mathbf{b}},\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
of non-edge barriers associated to
\mathbf{P}
are non-edge barriers
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{M}
such that
\widehat{\bf b}
lies in
\widehat{T}_{P}
,
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}
lies in
\widehat{T}_{P}^{\prime}
, and
\widehat{c}
lies in the closure of both
\widehat{\bf b}
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}
.

To simplify notation, for any oriented edge
\widehat{\bf e}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}
and any
{\bf k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
, we denote

[{\bf k},\widehat{\bf e}]:=\{({\bf k},\widehat{\bf e}),(\bar{\bf k},\widehat{\bar{\bf e}})\},

where recall that
\bar{\bf k}:=(k_{2},k_{1})
and
\widehat{\bar{\bf e}}
was defined by Notation
5.12
(1). Similarly, for every non-edge barrier
\widehat{\bf b}\in\mathcal{M}
and any
{\bf i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}
, we denote

[{\bf i},\widehat{\bf b}]:=\{({\bf i},\widehat{\bf b}),({\bf i}_{+},\widehat{\bf b}_{+}),({\bf i}_{-},\widehat{\bf b}_{-})\},

where
{\bf i}_{+}:=(i_{2},i_{3},i_{1})
,
{\bf i}_{-}:=(i_{3},i_{1},i_{2})
, and
\widehat{\bf b}_{+}
and
\widehat{\bf b}_{-}
were defined by Notation
5.12
(2).

Definition 7.3
.

For any marked pair of pants
\mathbf{P}
given by the pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
and any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
, let
(\widehat{\mathbf{b}},\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
be the pair of non-edge barriers associated to
\mathbf{P}
. Then define
\mu\in\mathscr{W}
to be the vector such that

•

\mu^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{f}}}=0
for all
(\mathbf{l},\widehat{\bf f})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{P}^{o}
,

•

\mu^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{2}&\text{if }(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in[\mathbf{i},\widehat{\mathbf{b}}];\\
-\frac{1}{2}&\text{if }(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in[\bar{\mathbf{i}},\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}];\\
0&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.

for all
(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}
.

The
\mathbf{i}
-
eruption vector field
associated to
\mathbf{P}
, denoted
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}
, is the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field associated to
\mu
, i.e.
{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}([\rho])\right)=\mu
for all
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, see Figure
15
. The
\mathbf{i}
-
eruption cocycle
associated to
\mathbf{P}
is the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\epsilon^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}:=\Psi\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}([\rho])\right)
.

For any
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}
, denote

\mathbf{i}^{\prime}:=(i_{1},i_{3},i_{2}).

Also, for any triple of integers
(a_{1},a_{3},a_{3})
, denote

\mathbf{i}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}):=(i_{1}+a_{1},i_{2}+a_{2},i_{3}+a_{3}).

Definition 7.4
.

For any marked pair of pants
\mathbf{P}
given by the pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
and any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
, let
(\widehat{\mathbf{b}},\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
be the pair of non-edge barriers associated to
\mathbf{P}
. Then define
\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}
by

•

\upsilon^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{f}}}={\upsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{f}}}=0
for all
(\mathbf{l},\widehat{\bf f})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{P}^{o}
,

•

\upsilon^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in[\mathbf{i}(0,1,-1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}]\cup[\mathbf{i}(-1,0,1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}]\cup[\mathbf{i}(1,-1,0),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}]\\
-1&\text{if }(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in[\mathbf{i}(0,-1,1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}]\cup[\mathbf{i}(1,0,-1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}]\cup[\mathbf{i}(-1,1,0),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}]\\
0&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.

for all
(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}
,

•

{\upsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}(0,-1,1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}]\cup[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}(1,0,-1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}]\cup[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}(-1,1,0),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}]\\
-1&\text{if }(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}(0,1,-1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}]\cup[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}(-1,0,1),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}]\cup[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}(1,-1,0),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}]\\
0&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.

for all
(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}
,

and define
\mu:=\upsilon+\upsilon^{\prime}
. The
\mathbf{i}
-
hexagon vector field
associated to
\mathbf{P}
, denoted
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}
, is the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field associated to
\mu
, i.e.
{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}([\rho])\right)=\mu
for all
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, see Figure
15
. The
\mathbf{i}
-
hexagon cocycle
associated to
\mathbf{P}
is the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\eta^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}:=\Psi\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}([\rho])\right)
.

Given a marked pair of pants
{\bf P}=(P,\widehat{c})
, let
\widehat{c}_{+}
and
\widehat{c}_{-}
be the other two boundary components of
P
, so that both
\widehat{\bf b}_{+}
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{-}
contain
\widehat{c}_{+}
in their closures, and both
\widehat{\bf b}_{-}
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}_{+}
contain
\widehat{c}_{-}
in their closures. Then let
{\bf P}_{+}:=(P,\widehat{c}_{+})
and
{\bf P}_{-}:=(P,\widehat{c}_{-})
, and note that

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{P}_{+}}^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{P}_{-}}^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{i}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{P}_{+}}^{\mathbf{i}_{+}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{P}_{-}}^{\mathbf{i}_{-}}.

for all
{\bf i}\in\mathcal{A}
. Thus, every
P\in\mathbb{P}
has
\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}
eruption labellings and
\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}
hexagon labellings associated to it.

Figure 15.
A non-isolated edge of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is drawn in red, a bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
across the non-isolated edge is drawn in green, and isolated edges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
are draw in blue. Each dot in an ideal triangle represents a triple
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
, and each dot along an edge represents a pair
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
. In the picture above, we have drawn a diagramatic representation for the eruption, hexagon, twist and lozenge labellings in turquoise (triangles), yellow (hexagons), grey (squares), and purple (discs) respectively.

Next, we describe the vectors
\mu
associated to the remaining two types of special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields, called the
twist vector fields
and
length vector fields
.

Definition 7.5
.

For any oriented, non-isolated edge
\widehat{\bf c}
in
\mathcal{P}^{o}
and any
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\mu\in\mathscr{W}
be the vector defined by

•

\mu^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{f}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\frac{1}{2}&\text{if }(\mathbf{l},\widehat{\mathbf{f}})\in[\mathbf{k},\widehat{\bf c}];\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

for all
(\mathbf{l},\widehat{\bf f})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{P}^{o}
,

•

\mu^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}=0
for all
(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}
.

The
\mathbf{k}
-
twist vector field
associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
, denoted
\mathcal{S}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
, is the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field associated to
\mu
, i.e.
{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}([\rho])\right)=\mu
for all
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, see Figure
15
. The
\mathbf{i}
-
twist cocycle
associated to
\mathbf{P}
is the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\sigma_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}:=\Psi\left(\mathcal{S}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}([\rho])\right)
.

Recall that for any
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
, we denote
\mathbf{k}_{1}:=(0,k_{1},k_{2})
,
\mathbf{k}_{2}:=(k_{2},0,k_{1})
,
\mathbf{k}_{3}:=(k_{1},k_{2},0)
,
\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{1}:=(0,k_{2},k_{1})
,
\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{2}:=(k_{1},0,k_{2})
, and
\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}:=(k_{2},k_{1},0)
.

Definition 7.6
.

For any oriented, non-isolated edge
\widehat{\bf c}
in
\mathcal{P}^{o}
and any
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
, let
P(1)
and
P(2)
be the pairs of pants in
\mathbb{P}
that share
\widehat{\bf c}
as a common boundary component (it is possible that
P(1)=P(2)
), such that
P(1)
and
P(2)
lie to the right and left of
\widehat{\bf c}
respectively. Also, for both
m=1,2
, let
{\bf P}(m):=(P(m),\widehat{c})
, and let
(\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(m),\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}(m))
be the pair of non-edge barriers associated to
{\bf P}(m)
. Then define
\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}
by

•

\upsilon^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{f}}}={\upsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{f}}}=0
for all
(\mathbf{l},\widehat{\bf f})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{P}^{o}
,

•

\upsilon^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}(1),{\bf k}_{3}]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}(1),{\bf k}_{3}(0,-1,1)];\\
1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}(2),\bar{\bf k}_{3}]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}(2),\bar{\bf k}_{3}(0,-1,1)];\\
-1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}(1),{\bf k}_{3}(1,-1,0)]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}(1),{\bf k}_{3}(-1,0,1)];\\
-1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}(2),\bar{\bf k}_{3}(1,-1,0)]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}(2),\bar{\bf k}_{3}(-1,0,1)];\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

for all
(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}
,

•

{\upsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(1),\bar{\bf k}_{2}]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(1),\bar{\bf k}_{2}(0,1,-1)];\\
1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(2),{\bf k}_{2}]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(2),{\bf k}_{2}(0,1,-1)];\\
-1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(1),\bar{\bf k}_{2}(1,0,-1)]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(1),\bar{\bf k}_{2}(-1,1,0)];\\
-1&\text{if }(\widehat{\bf d},{\bf j})\in[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(2),{\bf k}_{2}(1,0,-1)]\cup[\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}(2),{\bf k}_{2}(-1,1,0)];\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

for all
(\mathbf{j},\widehat{\mathbf{d}})\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{M}
,

and define
\mu:=\upsilon+\upsilon^{\prime}
.

(1)

The
\mathbf{k}
-
lozenge vector field
associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
, denoted
\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
, is the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field associated to
\mu
, i.e.
{\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}([\rho])\right)=\mu
for all
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, see Figure
15
. The
\mathbf{k}
-
lozenge cocycle
associated to
\mathbf{P}
is the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\zeta_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}:=\Psi\left(\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}([\rho])\right)
.

(2)

The
\mathbf{k}
-
length vector field
associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
, denoted
\mathcal{Y}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
, is the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field defined by

\mathcal{Y}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}:=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}+2\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{k}_{3}(0,-1,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(1)}-2\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{k}_{3}(-1,0,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(1)}+2\mathcal{E}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(0,-1,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(2)}-2\mathcal{E}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(-1,0,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(2)},

where the vector field
\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{k}_{3}(0,-1,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(1)}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{k}_{3}(-1,0,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(1)}
,
\mathcal{E}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(0,-1,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(2)}
, and
\mathcal{E}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(-1,0,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(2)}
) is defined to be the zero vector field if
\mathbf{k}_{3}(0,-1,1)
(resp.
\mathbf{k}_{3}(-1,0,1)
,
\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(0,-1,1)
,
\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(-1,0,1)
) does not lie in
\mathcal{B}
.
The
\mathbf{k}
-
length cocycle
associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
is the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}:=\Psi\left(\mathcal{Y}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}([\rho])\right)
.

Remark 7.7
.

In
[
SWZ20
]
, the
\mathbf{k}
-length vector field associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
was defined as

\mathcal{Y}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}:=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}+\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{k}_{3}(0,-1,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(1)}-\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{k}_{3}(-1,0,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(1)}+\mathcal{E}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(0,-1,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(2)}-\mathcal{E}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}_{3}(-1,0,1)}_{\mathbf{P}(2)}.

Notice that this is not the same as the definition given in Definition
7.6
. The corrected definition (given in Definition
7.6
) is required for Theorem
7.9
to hold.

Observe that for any oriented, non-isolated edge
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{T}^{o}
and any
\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

\mathcal{S}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{S}_{\widehat{\bar{\mathbf{c}}}}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}},\quad\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\bar{\mathbf{c}}}}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}},\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\mathcal{Y}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{Y}_{\widehat{\bar{\mathbf{c}}}}^{\bar{\mathbf{k}}}.

Thus, every
\widehat{c}\in\mathcal{P}
has
n-1
twist, and length vector fields associated to it.

Definition 7.8
.

Any eruption, hexagon, twist, or length vector field is a
special

(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field. Also, any eruption, hexagon, twist, or length cocycle is a
special

(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle.

There are
(n^{2}-1)(2g-2)
special admissible labellings in total. Our next and final goal is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be any pair of special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields and let
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
be any point. Let
\widehat{\bf c}\in\mathcal{P}^{o}
, and let
\mathbf{P}
be a marked pair of pants given by
\mathcal{P}
.

•

If
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}
, then

\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}};\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

•

If
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}
, then

\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}};\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

•

If
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}
, then

\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}};\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

•

If
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}
, then

\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}};\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Theorem
7.9
and Theorem
6.1
together imply the following corollary.

Corollary 7.10
.

The set of special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields, when evaluated at any
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, give a symplectic basis of
T_{[\rho]}\Hit_{V}(S)
.

For any special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
, let
\mathcal{X}^{*}
be the special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field that is dual to
\mathcal{X}
, i.e.
\omega(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathcal{X})=1
. By Theorem
7.9
,
\mathcal{X}^{*}
can be described explicitly as

\mathcal{X}^{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}};\\
-\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}};\\
\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}};\\
-\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}};\\
\end{array}\right.

In the companion paper
[
SWZ20
, Theorem 8.18 and Theorem 8.22]
, the authors computed, for every special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
, a real-analytic function
H(\mathcal{X}):\Hit_{V}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
with the defining property that the derivative of
H(\mathcal{X})
is
1
in the direction of
\mathcal{X}^{*}
, and
0
in the direction of any other special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field. These functions were written in terms of triple ratios and cross ratios of certain flags along the flag maps of Hitchin representations. As a consequence of Corollary
7.10
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 7.11
.

For any special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
, the function
H(\mathcal{X})
is the Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{X}
. Furthermore, the collection of functions

\{H(\mathcal{X}):\mathcal{X}\text{ is a special }(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})\text{-parallel vector field}\}

is a global Darboux coordinate system on
\Hit_{V}(S)
, i.e.

\omega=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{\mathbf{P}}{\rm d}(H(\mathcal{E}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}))\wedge{\rm d}(H(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}))+\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}}{\rm d}(H(\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{P}}))\wedge{\rm d}(H(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathbf{P}})).

7.3.
Internal and external parts

Since
\omega_{[\rho]}
is skew-symmetric, to prove Theorem
7.9
, it is sufficient to compute
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
in the following cases:

(1)

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is a twist vector field and
\mathcal{X}
is any special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field;

(2)

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is an eruption cocycle,
\mathcal{X}
is an eruption, hexagon or length vector field;

(3)

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is a hexagon cocycle,
\mathcal{X}
is a hexagon or length vector field;

(4)

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
are both length vector fields.

The computation of
\omega_{[\rho]}\big(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{X}\big)
for the above four cases is elementary but long. The goal of the rest of this paper is to perform this computation. As such, we fix once and for all the following data:

•

a Hitchin representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\PGL(V)
that represents
[\rho]
,

•

a triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
\Sigma
that is well-adapted to
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
, see Section
6.1
.

We will also choose an enumeration of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
and choose a representative in
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
for each triangle in
\mathbb{T}
as follows.

Recall that
\mathbb{T}
consists of two types of triangles, the internal triangles and the external triangles (see Definition
6.5
). Each pair of pants
P
of the pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}
contains two internal triangles and twelve external triangles. Let
\widehat{\delta}_{P}
be the internal triangle in
P
such that the ideal triangle of
\mathcal{T}
that contains
\widehat{\delta}_{P}
contains the endpoints of all the bridges that intersect
P
, and let
\widehat{\delta}_{P}^{\prime}
be the other internal triangle in
P
. Enumerate the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
by
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{12g-12}\}
such that the following holds:

•

If
v_{a}
,
v_{b}
, and
v_{c}
are the vertices of
\widehat{\delta}_{P}
with
a<b<c
, then the edges from
v_{a}
to
v_{b}
and from
v_{b}
to
v_{c}
are in the anti-clockwise direction about
\widehat{\delta}_{P}
.

•

If
v_{a}
is a vertex in the interior of a pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
and
v_{b}
is a vertex in a non-isolated edge in
\mathcal{P}
, then
a<b
.

•

If
v_{a}
and
v_{b}
are the two vertices on the same non-isolated edge in
\mathcal{P}
and
v_{a}
is the vertex that lies in a bridge parallel edge, then
a<b
.

Recall that
\pi_{S}:\widetilde{S}\to S
denotes the covering map. Choose triangles
\delta_{P}
and
\delta_{P}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
such that
\pi_{S}(\delta_{P})=\widehat{\delta}_{P}
and
\pi_{S}(\delta_{P}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\delta}_{P}^{\prime}
, so that
\delta_{P}
and
\delta_{P}^{\prime}
share a common vertex
v
with the property that if
v_{a}
,
v_{b}
, and
v_{c}
are the vertices of
\widehat{\delta}_{P}
with
a<b<c
, then the endpoints of
\pi_{S}(v)=v_{c}
. The union of these twelve external triangles in
P
are the three boundary cylinders of
P
, each of which is a union of four external triangles. Choose a lift to
\widetilde{S}
of each of these external triangles, such that

•

the lifts of the four external triangles in the same boundary cylinder of
P
lie in the same fundamental block, see Section
6.1
.

•

for any oriented, non-isolated edge
\widehat{\bf c}\in\mathcal{P}^{o}
, there is a lift
{\bf c}
of
\widehat{\bf c}
such that if
D(1)
is the fundamental block that contains the lifts of the four external triangles to the right of
\widehat{\bf c}
and
D(2)
is the fundamental block that contains the lifts of the four external triangles to the left of
\widehat{\bf c}
, then
\partial D(1)\cap{\bf c}
and
\partial D(2)\cap{\bf c}
are non-empty and coincide.

We have thus chosen a representative in
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
for each triangle in
\mathbb{T}
. Let
\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
be the collection of these choices, and let
\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{e}xt}
and
\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{i}nt}
denote the set of external and internal triangles in
\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
respectively.

For each triangle
\delta\in\mathbb{T}_{\rm lift}
, the enumeration we chose on the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
picks out a pair of edges
e_{\delta,1}
and
e_{\delta,2}
of
\delta
that are equipped with an induced orientation described in Section
6.1
. Then by (
2.9
) we may decompose

\displaystyle\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)=\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{ext}}}\sgn(\delta)

\displaystyle\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{int}}}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big),

where
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\nu}
are the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycle
\nu^{\prime}:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]))
and
\nu:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}([\rho]))
respectively.
We refer to the first and second sum on the right hand side above as the
internal
and
external
parts of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
respectively. We will compute these two parts separately in the following two sections.

Figure 16.
A non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is drawn in thick red, the isolated edges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
are drawn in blue, and the bridges in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
are drawn in dotted green. The edges of the
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-triangulation
\mathbb{T}
are drawn in orange. The bridge parallel edges of
\mathbb{T}
are the thicker orange lines.

7.4.
The internal part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)

The following notion is useful to calculate internal part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
P\in\mathbb{P}
be a pair of pants. Choose a boundary curve
c
of
P
, let
{\bf P}:=(P,c)
, and let
(\widehat{\mathbf{b}},\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
be the pair of non-edge barriers in
\mathcal{D}
associated to
\mathbf{P}
. A vector
\mu\in\mathscr{W}
is
symmetric (resp. skew-symmetric)
in
P
if
\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}=\mu^{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}
(resp.
\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}=-\mu^{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}
) for all
\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
. A
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field
\mathcal{X}
on
\Hit_{V}(S)
is
symmetric (resp. skew-symmetric)
in
P
if the vector
\mu:={\rm d}\Omega_{[\rho]}(\mathcal{X}([\rho]))
for some (any)
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
is symmetric (resp. skew-symmetric) in
P
.

By the symmetries of
\mu
, note that the notion of being symmetric or skew-symmetic in
P
does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{P}
. Observe that the eruption and twist vector fields are skew-symmetric in every pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
, and the twist, hexagon and lozenge vector fields are symmetric in every pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
.

The next lemma gives an easily verified condition that is useful for calculating the internal part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
in many cases.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
P
be a pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
, let
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
be
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields, and let
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\nu}
be the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles
\nu^{\prime}:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]))
and
\nu:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}([\rho]))
respectively.

(1)

If
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
are either both symmetric or both skew-symmetric in
P
, then

\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta_{P},1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta_{P},2})\big)=\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},2})\big).

In particular, if
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
are either both symmetric or both skew-symmetric in every pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
, then the internal part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
is zero.

(2)

If one of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{X}
is symmetric in
P
, while the other is skew-symmetric in
P
, then

\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta_{P},1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta_{P},2})\big)=-\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},2})\big)

Proof.

Let
c
be the boundary component of
P
such that if
(\widehat{\bf b},\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime})
is the pair of non-edge barriers associated to
{\bf P}:=(P,c)
, then there are (unique) lifts
{\bf b}
and
{\bf b}^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
of
\widehat{\bf b}
and
\widehat{\bf b}^{\prime}
respectively, such that
{\bf b}
intersects the interior of
e_{\delta_{P},1}
and
{\bf b}^{\prime}
intersects the interior of
e_{\delta_{P},2}
. Let
{\bf x}
be the triple associated to
{\bf b}
and let
{\bf x}^{\prime}
be the triple associated to
{\bf b}^{\prime}
.
By (
6.6
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta_{P},1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}-\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta_{P},2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}-\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{-}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{-})}+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}}\mu^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{-}}A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{-})}.

Lemma
6.7
(1) implies that for any
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
,

\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}_{+})}\right)=[\min\{i_{1}-j_{3},j_{1}-i_{2}\}]_{+}-\frac{i_{1}j_{1}}{n}=\tr\left(A^{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}\cdot A^{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}_{\xi_{\rho}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{-})}\right).

By definition, if
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
are either both symmetric in
P
or skew-symmetric in
P
, then

{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}\,\mu^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}={\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}\,\mu^{\mathbf{j}_{-}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}={\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}\,\mu^{(\mathbf{j}_{-})^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}={\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}\,\mu^{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{-}}

for all
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
, so (1) holds. On the other hand, when one of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{X}
is symmetric in
P
while the other is skew-symmetric in
P
, then

{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}}\,\mu^{\mathbf{j}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}=-{\mu^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}\,\mu^{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{-}}

for all
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\overline{\mathcal{B}}
, so (2) holds.
∎

7.5.
The external part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)

To calculate the external part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
, we consider each fundamental block separately.

For any fundamental block
D
, recall that
\mathbb{T}(D)
denote the four triangles in
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
that lie in
D
. By the way we enumerate the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
, note that all the crossing edges of
\mathbb{T}(D)
are oriented so that their forward endpoints lie in a non-isolated edge of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, and all the external edges of
\mathbb{T}(D)
are oriented so that their backward endpoints lie in a bridge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}
. However, the orientation of the internal edges of
\mathbb{T}(D)
depends on the position of the boundary cylinder
\pi(D)
relative to the enumeration of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
in the pair of pants containing
\pi(D)
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 7.14
.

Let
D
be a fundamental block and let
P
be the pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
containing the boundary cylinder
\pi(D)
. We say
D
is
consistent
if the boundary of
\pi(D)
contains edges of the form
\pi(e_{\delta_{P},l})
or
\pi(e_{\delta_{P}^{\prime},l})
, where
l=1,2
.

If we have three fundamental blocks in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
that project to the three different boundary cylinders of a pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
, then the enumeration of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
imply that of these three fundamental blocks, exactly two of them are consistent, and exactly one of them is inconsistent. When a fundamental block is consistent (resp. inconsistent), its internal edges are oriented so that their backward (resp. forward) endpoints lie in a bridge crossing edge.

To write down formulas, we use the following notation.

Notation 7.15
.

Let
D
be a fundamental block.

•

Let
u_{1}
and
u_{5}
be the bridge parallel edges in
D
, oriented so that their forward endpoints lie in a non-isolated edge, and
D
lies to the left of
u_{1}
and right of
u_{5}
.

•

Let
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{4}
be the four triangles whose union is
D
, such that
\delta_{1}
has
u_{1}
as an edge,
\delta_{4}
has
u_{5}
as an edge, and
\delta_{l}
and
\delta_{l+1}
share a common crossing edge for all
l=1,2,3
.

•

For
l=1,2,3
, let
u_{l+1}
denote the common edge shared by
\delta_{l}
and
\delta_{l+1}
, oriented so that its forward endpoint lie in a non-isolated edge.

•

For
l=1,\dots,4
, let
v_{l}
be the unique edge of
\delta_{l}
that is not a crossing edge, oriented so that their backward endpoints lie in the bridge crossing edge.

Figure 17.
On the left and right are consistent and inconsistent fundamental blocks respectively.

In this notation
u_{1},\dots,u_{5},v_{1}
, and
v_{4}
are oriented according to the enumeration of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
. However,
v_{2}
and
v_{3}
are oriented according to the enumeration of the vertices of
\mathbb{T}
if and only if
D
is consistent. From this, we see that

e_{\delta_{1},1}=u_{1},\,\,\,e_{\delta_{1},2}=v_{1},\,\,\,e_{\delta_{4},1}=u_{5},\,\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\,e_{\delta_{4},2}=v_{4}

for all fundamental blocks
D
. On the other hand,

e_{\delta_{2},1}=v_{2},\,\,\,e_{\delta_{3},1}=v_{3},\,\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\,e_{\delta_{2},2}=e_{\delta_{3},2}=u_{3}

if
D
is a consistent fundamental block, but

e_{\delta_{2},1}=-v_{2},\,\,\,e_{\delta_{2},2}=u_{2},\,\,\,e_{\delta_{3},1}=-v_{3},\,\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\,e_{\delta_{3},2}=u_{4}

if
D
is an inconsistent fundamental block, see Figure
17
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
be
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields, and let
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\nu}
be the
\Ad\circ\rho
-equivariant lift of the
(\rho,\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-tangent cocycles
\nu^{\prime}:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]))
and
\nu:=\Psi(\mathcal{X}([\rho]))
respectively. Let
{\bf c}
be the oriented, non-isolated edge in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{o}
such that
{\bf c}
intersects
\partial D
and
D
lies to the right of
{\bf c}
. Note that if
\mathbf{r}:=(r_{1},r_{2})
is the pair associated to
{\bf c}
, and
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
f_{i}\in\xi^{(i)}(r_{1})\cap\xi^{(n-i+1)}(r_{2})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, then for all
h\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{4}\}\cup\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
, both
\widetilde{\nu}(h)
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(h)
are represented in this basis by an upper triangular matrix. It is now an elementary computation to check that if
D
is consistent, then

(7.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{1}-v_{4})\big)+\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(u_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)+\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(-v_{2}+u_{1}+v_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big),

Similarly, if
D
is inconsistent, then

(7.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{1}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}^{2}(e_{\delta,2})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{1}(e_{\delta,1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}^{2}(e_{\delta,2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{1}-v_{4})\big)+\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(u_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)

\displaystyle+\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)-\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)+\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(-v_{2}+u_{1}+v_{1})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)

\displaystyle+\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)-\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big).

As a consequence, we have the following two lemmas.

Lemma 7.16
.

If
D
is a consistent fundamental block, and one of the following holds:

(1)

\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3}-v_{2})
are both nilpotent endomorphisms, or

(2)

\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})
are both nilpotent endomorphisms,

then

\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big)=0.

Proof.

This is obvious from (
7.1
).
∎

Lemma 7.17
.

If
D
is an inconsistent fundamental block, and one of the following holds:

(1)

\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3}-v_{2})
are both nilpotent endomorphisms, or

(2)

\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})\big)=\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})\big)
, and the endomorphisms
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})
are both nilpotent.

Then

\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{1}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}^{2}(e_{\delta,2})\big)=0.

Proof.

If both
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3}-v_{2})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3}-v_{2})
are nilpotent, then the diagonal parts of
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3})
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2})
(with respect to
{\bf r}
) agree, and the diagonal parts of
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})
agree, so (1) implies that
\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)=\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})_{{\bf r},{\rm diag}}\big)
as well. The lemma then follows from (
7.2
).
∎

In order to apply Lemmas
7.16
and
7.17
to the special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector fields, we use the following proposition. We omit its proof since it is a straightforward verification using Lemma
5.14
.

Proposition 7.18
.

Let
D
be a fundamental block, let
c\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
be the non-isolated edge that contains the external edges in
D
and let
\widehat{c}:=\pi(c)
.

(1)

If
\nu
is a twist cocycle, then
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})=0=\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})
. Further, if
\nu
is not associated to
\widehat{c}
, then
\widetilde{\nu}(u_{1})=0
as well.

(2)

If
\nu
is an eruption cocycle, then
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(u_{1})
are nilpotent endomorphisms.

(3)

If
\nu
is a hexagon cocycle, then
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})
is a nilpotent endomorphism. Moreover, if
\mathbf{P}:=(P,\widehat{c})
is a marked pair of pants, and
\nu=\eta^{\mathbf{i}}_{\mathbf{P}}
for some
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in\mathcal{B}
with
i_{3}>1
, then
\widetilde{\nu}(u_{1})
is also a nilpotent endomorphism.

(4)

If
\nu
is a lozenge cocycle not associated to
\widehat{c}
, then
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2})
,
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{3})
,
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})
, and
\widetilde{\nu}(u_{1})
are nilpotent endomorphisms.

7.6.
Explicit computations for the proof of Theorem
7.9

Using the tools we developed in Sections
7.4
and
7.5
, we will now compute
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
when
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{X})
satisfies of one of the four cases described at the start of Section
7.3
. The outcome of the computations in these four cases are described in the following four propositions. Together, they prove Theorem
7.9
.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
[\rho]\in\Hit_{V}(S)
, let
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}
for some oriented closed curve
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{P}^{o}
and some
\mathbf{k}:=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{X}
is any special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field. Then

\omega_{[\rho]}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho]))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}};\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

In this proof, we will use the following notation.

Notation 7.20
.

Let
\mathbf{l}:=(l_{1},l_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
. For any generic pair of flags
\mathbf{F}:=(F_{1},F_{2})
, let
M^{\mathbf{l}}_{\mathbf{F}}:V\to V
be the endomorphism that fixes the line
F_{1}^{(l_{1})}\cap F_{2}^{(l_{2}+1)}
, and whose kernel is
F_{1}^{(l_{1}-1)}+F_{2}^{(l_{2})}
.

Proof of Proposition
7.19
.

Observe that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is both symmetric and skew-symmetric in every pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
. Since every special
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{J})
-parallel vector field is either symmetric or skew-symmetric in every pair of pants in
\mathbb{P}
, Lemma
7.13
(1) implies that the internal part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
is zero. It is now sufficient to show that the external part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
is
1
when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{k}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}
, and is
0
otherwise. To do so, let
D
be a fundamental block, and let
u_{1},\dots,u_{5},v_{1},\dots,v_{4}
be as defined in Notation
7.15
. The proof proceeds in the following cases.

Case 1:
\mathcal{X}
is a twist, eruption or hexagon vector field.

By Proposition
7.18
(1)–(3), we know that both
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2}-v_{3})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})
are nilpotent, so Lemma
7.16
(1) and Lemma
7.17
(1) imply that

\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big)=0.

Since
D
is arbitrary, the external part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
is zero.

Case 2:
\mathcal{X}
is a length vector field not associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
.
If the boundary cylinder
\pi(D)
does not have
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
as a boundary component, then Proposition
7.18
(1) implies that
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(u_{1})=\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{3})=\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2})=0
. Then Lemma
7.16
(2) and Lemma
7.17
(2) imply that

\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big)=0.

Suppose that the boundary cylinder
\pi(D)
has
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
as a boundary component. Since
\mathcal{X}
is a length vector field that is not associated to
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
, Proposition
7.18
(1),(4) imply that
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(v_{2}-v_{3})
and
\widetilde{\nu}(v_{2}-v_{3})
are both nilpotent, so Lemma
7.16
(1) and Lemma
7.17
(1) gives

\sum_{\delta\in\mathbb{T}(D)}\sgn(\delta)\tr\big(\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}(e_{\delta,1})\cdot\widetilde{\nu}(e_{\delta,2})\big)=0.

Thus, the external part of
\omega_{[\rho]}\left(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}([\rho]),\mathcal{X}([\rho])\right)
is zero.

Case 3:
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbf{l}}_{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}
for some
